arrow presentations induces an isomorphism between the corresponding categories of (op)curves as well as an isomorphism of their path groupoids.

Using the term
P
-curve instead of “curve on
\Sigma
” is nevertheless advisable, especially if we want to define dual curves.
The dual (op)curves on
\Sigma
can be thought as (op)curves on the dual
\Sigma^{*}
. But if we want to see them as sequences of arrows
in
\Sigma
then we need a mapping
\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma^{*})\to\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)
. Such mappings are not unique as it is witnessed by the two duals
\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\sim$}}{\mathrm{P}}
and

\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}{\mathrm{P}}
of a presentation. So, if the context requires the precision, we shall speak about
\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\sim$}}{\mathrm{P}}
-(op)curves and
\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}{\mathrm{P}}
-(op)curves instead of
“dual (op)curves on
\Sigma
”.

Definition 5.2
.

A
\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\sim$}}{\mathrm{P}}
-opcurve is a sequence
\beta=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
of arrows
a_{i}\in\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)
such that
d_{0}a_{i}=d_{1}a_{i+1}
for
0<i<n
,
where we introduced the notation

d_{0}a:=\mathcal{O}_{2}(a),\qquad d_{1}a:=\mathcal{O}_{2}(T_{1}a).

The
\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}{\mathrm{P}}
-opcurves are defined as the reverses of
\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\sim$}}{\mathrm{P}}
-opcurves.

These definitions are based on the quivers

\operatorname{Qv}\bigl(\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\sim$}}{\mathrm{P}}\bigr)=\biggl(\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)\overset{d_{1}}{\underset{d_{0}}{\parbox{28.0pt}{\begin{picture}\put(3.0,6.0){\vector{1}{0}{22.0}}\put(3.0,2.0){\vector{1}{0}{22.0}}\end{picture}}}}\Sigma^{0}\biggr),\qquad\operatorname{Qv}\bigl(\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}{\mathrm{P}}\bigr)=\biggl(\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)\overset{d_{0}}{\underset{d_{1}}{\parbox{28.0pt}{\begin{picture}\put(3.0,6.0){\vector{1}{0}{22.0}}\put(3.0,2.0){\vector{1}{0}{22.0}}\end{picture}}}}\Sigma^{0}\biggr).

In fact, the
\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\sim$}}{\mathrm{P}}
-opcurves coincide with the
\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}{\mathrm{P}}
-curves and vice versa.

Remark 5.3
.

Passing from curves to opcurves, or back, can be automatized by introducing
{P^{\mathrm{op}}:=\bm{\langle}\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma),T_{1}T_{0}T_{1},T_{1}T_{2}T_{1}\bm{\rangle}}
which is a presentation isomorphic to
P

via the transformation
T_{1}
. Since
T_{1}
is also the involution of the quiver, so

\operatorname{Qv}(P^{\mathrm{op}})=\biggl(\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)\overset{\partial_{1}}{\underset{\partial_{0}}{\parbox{28.0pt}{\begin{picture}\put(3.0,6.0){\vector{1}{0}{22.0}}\put(3.0,2.0){\vector{1}{0}{22.0}}\end{picture}}}}\Sigma^{0}\biggr),

and therefore the
P^{\mathrm{op}}
-curves are the
P
-opcurves. Also, we have
\bigl(\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\sim$}}{\mathrm{P}}\bigr)^{\mathrm{op}}=\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}{\mathrm{P}}
. Although this might suggest a redundancy of using both curves and opcurves but
the concept of
P
-opcurve comprises not only the use of
P^{\mathrm{op}}
-curves but also the convention of using concatenation for their composition.

Now we turn to the description of curves on the dual of the double
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
of an OCPM
\Sigma
.
The dual of the double is particularly interesting for two reasons: Geometrically because it provides the Schreier coset graph for the
arrow presentation, and physically because it is the surface complex on which holonomy of the Kitaev model can be defined.

Fixing an arrow presentation
P=\bm{\langle}A,T_{0},T_{2}\bm{\rangle}
for
\Sigma
, the vertices of
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
can be identified with the set of sites, i.e.,
with pairs
\bm{\langle}v,f\bm{\rangle}\in\Sigma^{0}\times\Sigma^{2}
such that
v\cap f\neq\varnothing
, cf. (
2.4
) and Lemma
2.4
. The set of
arrows of
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
can be identified with the set
\mathbb{A}=\{a_{i}^{\sigma}\mid a\in A,\,i=0,2,\,\sigma=\pm\}
, see Figure
2
.
The meaning of the arrows can be made explicit by giving the source and target maps
\nabla_{0},\nabla_{1}\colon\mathbb{A}\to\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*0}
,

\begin{aligned} \nabla_{0}a_{0}^{+}&=\bm{\langle}\partial_{0}a,d_{1}a\bm{\rangle}\\
\nabla_{1}a_{0}^{+}&=\bm{\langle}\partial_{0}a,d_{0}a\bm{\rangle}\\
\nabla_{0}a_{2}^{+}&=\bm{\langle}\partial_{0}a,d_{0}a\bm{\rangle}\\
\nabla_{1}a_{2}^{+}&=\bm{\langle}\partial_{1}a,d_{0}a\bm{\rangle}\end{aligned}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\begin{aligned} \nabla_{0}a_{i}^{-}&=\nabla_{1}a_{i}^{+}\\
\nabla_{1}a_{i}^{-}&=\nabla_{0}a_{i}^{+}.\end{aligned}

(5.1)

The quiver
\mathbb{A}\overset{\nabla_{0}}{\underset{\nabla_{1}}{\parbox{28.0pt}{\begin{picture}\put(3.0,6.0){\vector{1}{0}{22.0}}\put(3.0,2.0){\vector{1}{0}{22.0}}\end{picture}}}}\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*0}
can be seen to be the quiver associated to the arrow presentation (
4.5
) so we call it
\operatorname{Qv}(\mathcal{D}(P)^{\sim})
. Curves and opcurves on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
will always be meant with respect to this quiver.

Lemma 5.4
.

The following map embeds the involutive quiver of
\mathcal{D}(P)^{\sim}
to the cartesian product of the involutive quivers of
P
and
\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\sim$}}{\mathrm{P}}
:

\displaystyle\biggl(\mathbb{A}\overset{\nabla_{0}}{\underset{\nabla_{1}}{\parbox{28.0pt}{\begin{picture}\put(3.0,6.0){\vector{1}{0}{22.0}}\put(3.0,2.0){\vector{1}{0}{22.0}}\end{picture}}}}\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*0}\biggr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle E}}{{\longrightarrow}}\biggl(A\overset{\partial_{0}}{\underset{\partial_{1}}{\parbox{28.0pt}{\begin{picture}\put(3.0,6.0){\vector{1}{0}{22.0}}\put(3.0,2.0){\vector{1}{0}{22.0}}\end{picture}}}}\Sigma^{0}\biggr)\times\biggl(A\overset{d_{1}}{\underset{d_{0}}{\parbox{28.0pt}{\begin{picture}\put(3.0,6.0){\vector{1}{0}{22.0}}\put(3.0,2.0){\vector{1}{0}{22.0}}\end{picture}}}}\Sigma^{2}\biggr),

\displaystyle\bm{\langle}v,f\bm{\rangle}

\displaystyle\mapsto\bm{\langle}v,f\bm{\rangle},

\displaystyle a_{0}^{+}

\displaystyle\mapsto\bm{\langle}\partial_{0}a,a\bm{\rangle},

\displaystyle a_{0}^{-}

\displaystyle\mapsto\bm{\langle}\partial_{0}a,T_{1}a\bm{\rangle},

\displaystyle a_{2}^{+}

\displaystyle\mapsto\bm{\langle}a,d_{0}a\bm{\rangle},

\displaystyle a_{2}^{-}

\displaystyle\mapsto\bm{\langle}T_{1}a,d_{0}a\bm{\rangle}.

Proof.

Recall that the cartesian product of quivers
\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathbb{Q^{\prime}}
has set of vertices
(\mathbb{Q}\times\mathbb{Q^{\prime}})^{0}=\mathbb{Q}^{0}\times\mathbb{Q^{\prime}}^{0}

and set of arrows
(\mathbb{Q}\times\mathbb{Q^{\prime}})^{1}=\bigl(\mathbb{Q}^{0}\times\mathbb{Q^{\prime}}^{1}\bigr)\sqcup\bigl(\mathbb{Q}^{1}\times\mathbb{Q^{\prime}}^{0}\bigr)
and it is endowed with the obvious
source and target maps.
In case of the quivers of
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{*}
, these obvious maps are
\partial_{0}\times d_{1}
and
\partial_{1}\times d_{0}
with the warning that here the
\partial_{i}
and

d_{i}
are used in a generalized sense: The
\partial_{i}
is extended to the trivial edges of
\Sigma
, i.e., to the vertices, by
\partial_{0}v=\partial_{1}v=v
and the

d_{i}
is extended to the trivial edges of
\Sigma^{*}
, i.e., to the faces, by
d_{0}f=d_{1}f=f
. Then the fact that
E
is a homomorphism of
involutive quivers,

\displaystyle(\partial_{0}\times d_{1})\,\circ\,E=E\,\circ\,\nabla_{0},\qquad(\partial_{1}\times d_{0})\,\circ\,E=E\,\circ\,\nabla_{1},\qquad(T_{1}\times\tilde{T}_{1})\,\circ\,E=E\,\circ\,\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}

can be easily verified. That
E
is injective is also clear.
∎

In the image of
E
the arrows can be visualized as “isosceles triangles”. The triangle
E\bigl(a_{2}^{\pm}\bigr)
has base edge
a
with opposite vertex being the face

f=d_{0}a
and the legs of the triangle being the sites
\bm{\langle}v,f\bm{\rangle}
where
v
is either
\partial_{0}a
or
\partial_{1}a
. The triangles
E\bigl(a_{0}^{\pm}\bigr)
are called
dual triangles because in them the roles of vertices and faces are interchanged. If
\rho=(d_{j})
is a curve on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
, then two
consecutive triangles
E(d_{j})
and
E(d_{j+1})
are pasted together at a leg so the whole picture of the curve looks like a ribbon with the base
edges of triangles forming one borderline and that of the dual triangles (drawn as edges of
\Sigma^{*}
) forming the other borderline.

Figure 3:
Every curve
\rho
(black) on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
is accompanied by a curve
\gamma
(blue) on
\Sigma
and a curve
\beta
(red) on
\Sigma^{*}

according to Lemma
5.4
. They border a strip made of triangles but the strip may fail to be embedded into the surface of
\Sigma
:
The
\gamma
and
\beta
intersect locally, i.e., the strip folds, precisely if the
\rho
makes a turn around a type 1 (green) face of
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
.

On the two ends, we see two unpaired legs of some triangles and the texture of the ribbon itself is a triangulated surface, see
Figure
3
.
The 2 borderline curves can be obtained by writing
E(d_{j})
as
\bm{\langle}c_{j},b_{j}\bm{\rangle}
and then
\gamma=(c_{j})
is a
P
-curve and
\beta=(b_{j})
is a
\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\sim$}}{\mathrm{P}}
-curve
in the generalized sense:
\gamma
may contain vertices and
\beta
may contain faces. But
c_{j}
is a vertex if and only if
b_{j}
is a genuine arrow and

c_{j}
is a genuine arrow if and only if
b_{j}
is a face, hence the pair
\bm{\langle}c_{j},b_{j}\bm{\rangle}
is always a triangle.

This is the origin of the names such as “triangle operators” and “ribbon curves” in the literature on the Kitaev model, although the name
ribbon curve is usually reserved for a special kind of curve on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
which we will define below.

In the next lemma, we take a closer look into the structure of
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
. We will see that we can forget about the complicated arrow presentation
(
4.5
) and navigate through
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
using essentially only the arrow presentation of
\Sigma
.

Lemma 5.5
.

The map
a\mapsto s(a):=\bm{\langle}\mathcal{O}_{0}(a),\mathcal{O}_{2}(a)\bm{\rangle}
is a bijection
\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)\cong\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*0}
.
Every vertex of
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
has degree
4
and the four arrows pointing out from
s(a)
can be labelled, or colored, by the letters

\bigl\{T_{0},T_{0}^{-1},T_{2},T_{2}^{-1}\bigr\}
as follows

\displaystyle(T_{0}a)_{0}^{+}\colon\

\displaystyle s(a)\to s(T_{0}a)\qquad

\displaystyle\text{labelled by }T_{0},

\displaystyle a_{0}^{-}\colon\

\displaystyle s(a)\to s\bigl(T_{0}^{-1}a\bigr)\qquad

\displaystyle\text{labelled by }T_{0}^{-1},

\displaystyle a_{2}^{+}\colon\

\displaystyle s(a)\to s(T_{2}a)\qquad

\displaystyle\text{labelled by }T_{2},

\displaystyle\bigl(T_{2}^{-1}a\bigr)_{2}^{-}\colon\

\displaystyle s(a)\to s\bigl(T_{2}^{-1}a\bigr)\qquad

\displaystyle\text{labelled by }T_{2}^{-1}

.

The order in which they are listed corresponds to the cyclic order induced by the action of
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{0}
of (
4.5
). Moreover,
if an arrow
d
carries the label
T_{j}^{\epsilon}
then
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}d
has label
T_{j}^{-\epsilon}
.

Proof.

\mathcal{O}_{0}(a)\cap\mathcal{O}_{2}(a)=\{a\}
is non-empty, so
s(a)
is a site, cf. Definition
2.5
and (
2.4
). If
\bm{\langle}v,f\bm{\rangle}
is a site, then

v\cap f\neq\varnothing
, so
v=\mathcal{O}_{0}(a)
and
f=\mathcal{O}_{2}(a)

for the unique element
a\in v\cap f
. So
s
is indeed a bijection.

The four arrows of source
s(a)
are dual to the 4 boundary arrows of the rhombus face
s(a)
of the double
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)
. (The reason for drawing
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)
and not

\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
is merely typographical.)

\parbox{120.0pt}{\begin{picture}\polyline(20,0)(80,60)(60,80)(0,20)(20,0)
\polyline(60,0)(80,20)(20,80)(0,60)(60,0)
\color{melegzold}
\put(20.0,0.0){\circle*{4.0}}
\put(60.0,0.0){\circle*{4.0}}
\put(20.0,40.0){\circle*{4.0}}
\put(60.0,40.0){\circle*{4.0}}
\put(20.0,80.0){\circle*{4.0}}
\put(60.0,80.0){\circle*{4.0}}
\put(7.0,36.0){$e^{L}$}
\put(65.0,36.0){$e^{R}$}
\color{blue}
\put(40.0,20.0){\circle*{4.0}}
\put(0.0,60.0){\circle*{4.0}}
\put(80.0,60.0){\circle*{4.0}}
\put(38.0,25.0){$v$}
\put(-13.0,56.0){$v^{L}$}
\put(83.0,56.0){$v^{R}$}
\color{red}
\put(0.0,20.0){\circle*{4.0}}
\put(80.0,20.0){\circle*{4.0}}
\put(40.0,60.0){\circle*{4.0}}
\put(37.0,49.0){$f$}
\put(-13.0,18.0){$f^{L}$}
\put(83.0,18.0){$f^{R}$}
\color{black}
\put(43.0,19.0){\vector{1}{1}{40.0}} \put(78.0,46.0){$a$}
\color{black}
\put(33.0,38.0){$\scriptstyle s(a)$}
\put(8.0,18.0){$\scriptstyle s(T_{0}a)$}
\put(48.0,18.0){$\scriptstyle s(T_{0}^{-1}a)$}
\put(6.0,58.0){$\scriptstyle s(T_{2}^{-1}a)$}
\put(49.0,58.0){$\scriptstyle s(T_{2}a)$}
\end{picture}}\qquad\begin{aligned} &v=\mathcal{O}_{0}(a),\qquad&&f=\mathcal{O}_{2}(a)&\\
&e^{L}=\mathcal{O}_{1}(T_{0}a),\qquad&&e^{R}=\mathcal{O}_{1}(a)&\\
&v^{L}=\mathcal{O}_{0}\bigl(T_{2}^{-1}a\bigr),\qquad&&v^{R}=\mathcal{O}_{0}(T_{1}a)&\\
&f^{L}=\mathcal{O}_{2}(T_{0}a),\qquad&&f^{R}=\mathcal{O}_{2}(T_{1}a).&\end{aligned}

(5.2)

The
e^{L}
,
v
,
e^{R}
,
f
are the 4 vertices of the rhombus by (
2.5
),
\bigl\{v,v^{L}\bigr\}=\operatorname{Bd}\bigl(e^{L}\bigr)
,
\bigl\{v,v^{R}\bigr\}=\operatorname{Bd}\bigl(e^{R}\bigr)
,

\bigl\{f,f^{L}\bigr\}=\operatorname{Cb}\bigl(e^{L}\bigr)
,
\bigl\{f,f^{R}\bigr\}=\operatorname{Cb}\bigl(e^{R}\bigr)
and the four neighbour sites of
\bm{\langle}v,f\bm{\rangle}
are
\bigl\bm{}{\langle}v,f^{L}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}
,
\bigl\bm{}{\langle}v,f^{R}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}
,
\bigl\bm{}{\langle}v^{R},f\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}
and

\bigl\bm{}{\langle}v^{L},f\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}
. Computing the intersections of the vertex and face within these sites, we recognize them as
s(T_{0}a)
,
s\bigl(T_{0}^{-1}a\bigr)
,
s(T_{2}a)

and
s\bigl(T_{2}^{-1}a\bigr)
, respectively.
(
For example,
v\cap f^{L}=\mathcal{O}_{0}(a)\cap\mathcal{O}_{2}(T_{0}a)=\mathcal{O}_{0}(T_{0}a)\cap\mathcal{O}_{2}(T_{0}a)=\{T_{0}a\}
.
)

This partially explains the labels of the four arrows, partially because some of these 4 sites can be identical. (Indeed,
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
may have double arrows but never
multiple arrows of larger multiplicity.)

s(T_{0}a)=s\bigl(T_{0}^{-1}a\bigr)
occurs when
v
has degree 2 and
s(T_{2}a)=s\bigl(T_{2}^{-1}a\bigr)
occurs when
f
is a 2-gon. In these degenerate cases

or

there is a double
arrow from
s(a)
to, let’s say,
s(T_{2}a)
(the 2nd picture above) and it seems to be indifferent which is labelled by
T_{2}
because
T_{2}
acts on
a
as
T_{2}^{-1}
.
However, the letters
T_{2}
and
T_{2}^{-1}
are different and so are the two parallel arrows. The labelling in this case is defined by forcing the lemma:
The labels must follow the cyclic order
\big[T_{0},T_{0}^{-1},T_{2},T_{2}^{-1}\big]
when we apply
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{0}
.
There is only one case left. If both types of degeneracy occur for the same arrow
a
, then
T_{0}^{2}a=a=T_{2}^{2}a
so by Example
2.10
the connected component
of
a
is nothing but the minimal OCPM,
\Sigma_{\mathrm{min}}
. In this case, the correctness of the labelling can be checked by inspection.
∎

The above lemma has two important consequences. The first one is an interpretation of
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
as the coset graph of the arrow presentation of
\Sigma
.
This is the next corollary. The second one is a useful encoding of the curves on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
in terms of words in the alphabet
\bigl\{T_{0},T_{0}^{-1},T_{2},T_{2}^{-1}\bigr\}
, see
the definition below.

Corollary 5.6
.

Let
\Sigma
be a connected OCPM. Every arrow presentation
\bm{\langle}\mathrm{Arr},T_{0},T_{2}\bm{\rangle}
of
\Sigma
determines a transitive action on
\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)
of the

\Sigma
-independent group
\mathcal{T}=\bm{\langle}T_{0},T_{2}\mid T_{0}T_{2}T_{0}T_{2}\bm{\rangle}
and therefore the arrow presentation can be thought as a presentation of the left
coset space
\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{H}
of the group
\mathcal{T}
with respect to some subgroup. Then the Schreier coset graph of
\mathcal{T}/\mathcal{H}
can be drawn on
the surface
[\Sigma]
as follows. Each
a\in\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)

is drawn as a point at
s(a)\in\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*0}
. For each
a\in\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)
, we draw two arrows:
(T_{0}a)_{0}^{+}\colon s(a)\to s(T_{0}a)
colored blue and

a_{2}^{+}\colon s(a)\to s(T_{2}a)
colored red. Then every edge of
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
is drawn exactly once. The blue arrows represent the flow of the permutation

T_{0}
and the red ones represent that of
T_{2}
. In this sense drawing the dual of the double we draw the arrow presentation of
\Sigma

(
Figure
\ref{fig: flow})
.

Figure 4:
The dual of the double of an OCPM
\Sigma
provides the Schreier coset graph of the arrow presentation of
\Sigma
. The blue and red arrows
show the action of
T_{0}
and
T_{2}
, respectively. The green quadrangles represent the universal relation
T_{0}T_{2}T_{0}T_{2}=\operatorname{id}
.

Definition 5.7
.

Using the function
s\colon\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)\to\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*0}
defined in the above lemma a curve
\rho=(d_{n},\dots,d_{1})
on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
can be encoded by the pair

\bm{\langle}a_{0},w\bm{\rangle}\in\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)\times\bigl\{T_{0},T_{0}^{-1},T_{2},T_{2}^{-1}\bigr\}^{*},

where
s(a_{0})=\nabla_{0}d_{1}
is the source vertex of the curve,
w=W_{n}\cdots W_{1}
is the code word made of letters
W_{j}\in\bigl\{T_{0},T_{0}^{-1},T_{2},T_{2}^{-1}\bigr\}

in such a way that for
0<j\leq n
the
d_{j}
is the unique arrow of source
s(a_{j-1})
and label
W_{j}
, so its target can be written as
s(a_{j})
with

a_{j}=W_{j}a_{j-1}
.

The same pair
\bm{\langle}a_{0},w\bm{\rangle}
is used to encode the opcurve
\rho^{\mathrm{rev}}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})
.

Now we are in a position to define ribbons.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
\rho=(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})
be either a curve or an opcurve on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
with encoding
\bm{\langle}a_{0},w\bm{\rangle}
.
The following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

The triangles
E(d_{j})
and
E(d_{j+1})
are non-overlapping
(
their interiors are disjoint
)
for
0<j<n
.

(ii)

\rho
is reduced and the
2
borderlines
\gamma
and
\beta
of the “ribbon”
\rho
do not intersect locally, i.e.,
\mathcal{O}_{1}(b_{j})\neq\mathcal{O}_{1}(c_{j\pm 1})

whenever both
b_{j}
and
c_{j\pm 1}
are non-trivial arrows.

(iii)

\rho
is reduced and if
(d_{j},d_{j+1})
is a left or a right turn (not straight), then the unique face it turns around is either a type
0
face
or a type
2
face, never a type
1
face.

(iv)

The word
w
is reduced and does not contain
T_{0}T_{2}
,
T_{2}T_{0}
and their inverses.

(v)

The word
w
belongs to either
\bigl\{T_{0}^{-1},T_{2}\bigr\}^{*}
or
\bigl\{T_{0},T_{2}^{-1}\bigr\}^{*}
.

Proof.

Let
\bm{\langle}v,f\bm{\rangle}=\nabla_{0}d_{j+1}=\nabla_{1}d_{j}
. Then the triangles
E(d_{j})
and
E(d_{j+1})
contain
\bm{\langle}v,f\bm{\rangle}
as a leg.
There are 4 such triangles. In the notations of (
5.2
), they are
vfv^{L}
,
fvf^{L}
,
vfv^{R}
and
fvf^{R}
. The first two and the second two
overlap because their base edges intersect at their “middle” points
e^{L}
and
e^{R}
, respectively. This intersection is, at the same time, an intersection
of the two borderlines
\gamma
and
\beta
of the ribbon.
No other pairs of triangles are overlapping except when the two triangles are the same, this happens precisely when
d_{j+1}=\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}d_{j}
.

In case of intersection at
e^{L}
the
(d_{j},d_{j+1})
is either
\bigl(\bigl(T_{2}^{-1}a\bigr)_{2}^{+},(T_{0}a)_{0}^{+}\bigr)
or its inverse and the corresponding code word is

T_{0}T_{2}
or its inverse.
In case of intersection at
e^{R}
, the
(d_{j},d_{j+1})
is either
\bigl(a_{0}^{+},a_{2}^{+}\bigr)
or its inverse and the corresponding code word is

T_{2}T_{0}
or its inverse. These pairs of arrows make turn around the type 1 face
e^{L}
and
e^{R}
, respectively. All other pairs of arrows are either straight
or make a turn around
v
or
f
.

If a reduced word
w
does not contain the alphabet changing words
T_{0}T_{2}
,
T_{2}T_{0}
and their inverses, then
W_{1}\in\bigl\{T_{0}^{-1},T_{2}\bigr\}
is equivalent to

w\in\bigl\{T_{0}^{-1},T_{2}\bigr\}^{*}
. The same holds for the alphabet
\bigl\{T_{0},T_{2}^{-1}\bigr\}
.
∎

Definition 5.9
.

A ribbon (op)curve is a (op)curve on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
satisfying the conditions of Lemma
5.8
.
Let
\mathsf{Ribb}(\Sigma)
and
\mathsf{Ribb}^{\circ}(\Sigma)
be the set of ribbon curves and ribbon opcurves, respectively.
If the code word of a ribbon (op)curve belongs to
\bigl\{T_{0}^{-1},T_{2}\bigr\}^{*}
, then it is called a left ribbon (op)curve.
If it belongs to
\bigl\{T_{0},T_{2}^{-1}\bigr\}^{*}
, we speak about right ribbon (op)curve.
Let
\mathsf{Ribb}_{L}(\Sigma)
,
\mathsf{Ribb}_{R}(\Sigma)
,
\mathsf{Ribb}^{\circ}_{L}(\Sigma)
,
\mathsf{Ribb}^{\circ}_{R}(\Sigma)
be the set of left/right ribbon curves/opcurves, respectively.

Since every ribbon is reduced,
\mathsf{Ribb}(\Sigma)
embeds into the path groupoid
\mathsf{Path}(\Sigma)
. But it is not a subgroupoid and not even a subcategory
as one can infer from the characterization Lemma
5.8
(v). The
\mathsf{Ribb}_{L}(\Sigma)
and
\mathsf{Ribb}_{R}(\Sigma)
, however, are subcategories with
the inverse of every left ribbon being a right ribbon. Moreover,
\mathsf{Ribb}(\Sigma)=\mathsf{Ribb}_{L}(\Sigma)\cup\mathsf{Ribb}_{R}(\Sigma)
.
Analogous remarks can be made for the ribbon opcurves.

The above definition of ribbons seems to agree with that of
[
1
]
but it is less restrictive than that of
[
15
]
.
The latter type of ribbons will appear here as proper ribbons in Definition
6.1
. The necessity of distinguishing two kinds of ribbons
has been realized already in
[
22
]
, where they are called locally clockwise and locally counterclockwise. Our terminology of left and right refers to that whether
the red dual curve on Figure
3
is on the left or on the right of the blue curve.
So ribbons may also be described as curves on
\Sigma
together with a consistent framing by a dual curve. It is a left ribbon if and only if the framing goes, and stays, on the left-hand side.

6
Holonomy

According to
[
15
,
16
]
, the holonomy of a Hopf algebra gauge theory on
\Sigma
over the quasitriangular Hopf algebra
Q
is a
groupoid homomorphism

\operatorname{Hol}\colon\ \mathsf{Path}(\Sigma)\to\operatorname{Hom}_{K}^{\times}\biggl(Q^{*},\bigotimes_{e\in\Sigma^{1}}Q^{*}\biggr),

where the
\operatorname{Hom}_{K}(C,A)
for a coalgebra
C
and algebra
A
is the convolution algebra
[
20
]
and
\operatorname{Hom}_{K}^{\times}(C,A)
refers to the group of
convolution invertible elements of
\operatorname{Hom}_{K}(C,A)
.
For a gauge theory over
\mathcal{D}(H)
we must choose
Q=\mathcal{D}(H)
. But since we need holonomy in the Kitaev model merely for the purpose of constructing string operators,
we will follow the above recipe very loosely and use the name holonomy for any groupoid homomorphism

\operatorname{Hol}\colon\ \mathsf{Path}(\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{K}^{\times}(\mathcal{D}(H)^{*},\mathcal{M}(\Sigma)).

The convolution product of two functions
f,g\colon\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}\to\mathcal{M}
is
f\ast g(\Psi)=f\bigl(\Psi_{(1)}\bigr)g\bigl(\Psi_{(2)}\bigr)
for
\Psi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
and the holonomy of a path

(d_{n},\dots,d_{1})
on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
is uniquely determined by the holonomies of the length 1 paths by

\operatorname{Hol}_{(d_{n},\dots,d_{1})}=\operatorname{Hol}_{(d_{n})}\ast\cdots\ast\operatorname{Hol}_{(d_{2})}\ast\operatorname{Hol}_{(d_{1})}.

(6.1)

In order for this formula to depend only on the path of the curve the length 1, holonomies should satisfy that
\operatorname{Hol}_{(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}d)}

be the convolution inverse of
\operatorname{Hol}_{(d)}
. If the function
\operatorname{Hol}_{(d)}\colon{\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}\to\mathcal{M}}
happens to be either an algebra homomorphism or an antialgebra
homomorphism, then its convolution inverse is simply
\operatorname{Hol}_{(d)}\,\circ\,S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}
.

We shall use also opholonomies

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}\colon\ \mathsf{Path}(\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\operatorname{Hom}_{K}^{\times}(\mathcal{D}(H)^{*},\mathcal{M}(\Sigma)),

which can be defined as functions on opcurves
(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})
by

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d_{1})}\ast\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d_{2})}\ast\cdots\ast\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d_{n})}.

(6.2)

The condition of descending to the paths of opcurves is again that
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}d)}
be the convolution inverse of
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d)}
.

The ribbon operators of
[
1
]
, as well as the symbolic arrows (
3.31
), suggest that opholonomy and holonomy
should be defined by

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d)}(h\otimes\varphi):=\begin{cases}\varphi(1)P_{a}(h)&\text{if}\ d=a_{0}^{+},\\
\varepsilon(h)Q_{a}(\varphi)&\text{if}\ d=a_{2}^{+},\\
\varphi(1)P_{a}(S(h))&\text{if}\ d=a_{0}^{-},\\
\varepsilon(h)Q_{a}(S(\varphi))&\text{if}\ d=a_{2}^{-}\end{cases}

(6.3)

for all
a\in\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)
and

\operatorname{Hol}_{(d)}:=S_{\mathcal{M}}\,\circ\,\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d)}\,\circ\,S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}},\qquad d\in\mathrm{Arr}(\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}),

(6.4)

where
S_{\mathcal{M}}
is the involutive algebra automorphism
\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
which sends
P_{a}(h)
to
P_{T_{1}a}(h)
and
Q_{a}(\varphi)
to
Q_{T_{1}a}(\varphi)
.
By (
3.1
), the
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d)}
are antialgebra maps for
d=a_{0}^{+}
and algebra maps for
d=a_{2}^{+}
. Moreover, in spite of the complicated
expression (
3.3
) it is easy to see that
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}d)}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d)}\,\circ\,S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}
. So (
6.3
) together with
(
6.2
) defines indeed an opholonomy. Then (
6.4
) together with (
6.1
) is automatically a holonomy.

The relation (
6.4
) between holonomy and opholonomy is rather arbitrary. We could have defined them in such a way that
\operatorname{Hol}_{(d)}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d)}

for all arrows
d
of
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
. The choice (
6.4
) perhaps allows easier comparison with the existing definitions of the literature.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\gamma=(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})
be a (op)curve on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
with
d_{k}=(a_{k})^{\sigma_{k}}_{i_{k}}
. Then

\gamma
is called proper if for all
1\leq k<l\leq n
the following statements hold:
a_{k}\neq a_{l}
and if
a_{k}=T_{1}a_{l}
then
i_{k}=i_{l}
.
In other words, no pairs
d_{k}
,
d_{l}
of arrows in the (op)curve are equal or incident and lie on the boundary of the same type 1 face
e\in\Sigma^{1}
.

Proposition 6.2
.

The relation of proper
(
op
)
curves to ribbons is the following:

(i)

Every proper curve
\gamma=(d_{n},\dots,d_{1})
on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
is a ribbon curve and

\operatorname{Hol}_{\gamma}=S_{\mathcal{M}}\,\circ\,\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma^{\mathrm{rev}}}\,\circ\,S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}=S_{\mathcal{M}}\,\circ\,\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma^{\mathrm{rev}-1}}.

(ii)

A ribbon opcurve
\rho=(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})
on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
is proper if and only if it is a simple opcurve. So proper ribbon and simple ribbon are synonymous.

Proof.

(i) Notice that an opposite pair
d
,
\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}d
is always of the form
a_{i}^{+}
,
a_{i}^{-}
.
Therefore, proper (op)curves are reduced. Hence, the property of being proper for consecutive arrows
d_{k}
,
d_{k+1}
is equivalent to
the property of Lemma
5.8
(iii). This proves that proper (op)curves are ribbons.
The property of being proper for any pair
d_{k}
,
d_{l}
implies that the
P
or
Q
operators associated to them by the opholonomy commute:

\bigl[\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d_{k})}(\Phi),\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d_{l})}(\Psi)\bigr]=0
for every
\Phi,\Psi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
and for
k\neq l
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}_{\gamma}(\Psi)=S_{\mathcal{M}}\bigl(\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d_{1})}\bigl(S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}\bigl(\Psi_{(n)}\bigr)\bigr)\cdots\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d_{n})}\bigl(S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}\bigl(\Psi_{(1)}\bigr)\bigr)\bigr)=S_{\mathcal{M}}\bigl(\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma^{\mathrm{rev}}}(S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}(\Psi))\bigr).

This proves the first equation. The second follows from the first by the observation that for a proper opcurve
\alpha
the

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\alpha}\,\circ\,S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}
is the convolution inverse of
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\alpha}
hence equal to the opholonomy of
\alpha^{-1}
.

(ii) Assume
\rho
is not simple. Then there exists a site
s
and a minimal closed subcurve
(d_{i},d_{i+1},\dots,d_{j-1})\colon s\to s
of
\rho
. Here
i=1
or
j=n+1
is allowed
but not both because this would imply that
\rho
is closed and simple. If
i>1
, then
d_{i-1}
and
d_{j-1}
are incident to
s
and lie on the boundary of a type 1 face.
If
j\leq n
, then the same holds for the arrows
d_{i}
and
d_{j}
. Anyway, we get contradiction with properness of
\rho
.

If
\rho
is not proper, then for some
1\leq k<l\leq n
the
d_{k}=(a_{k})^{\sigma_{k}}_{i_{k}}
and
d_{l}=(a_{l})^{\sigma_{l}}_{i_{l}}
satisfy one of the following: either
a_{k}=a_{l}
or
(
a_{k}=T_{1}a_{l}
and
i_{k}\neq i_{l}
). In both cases
\rho
is either not simple or not a ribbon.
∎

En passant, we mention a useful rewriting of the exchange relations of the
P
and
Q
operators in terms of length 1 opholonomies:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{0}^{-})}(\Psi)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{2}^{+})}(\Phi)=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{2}^{+})}(\Phi\leftharpoonup R_{1})\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{0}^{-})}(\Psi\leftharpoonup R_{2}),

(6.5)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{((T_{1}a)_{2}^{+})}(\Psi)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{0}^{-})}(\Phi)=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{0}^{-})}(R_{2}\rightharpoonup\Phi)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{((T_{1}a)_{2}^{+})}(R_{1}\rightharpoonup\Psi),

(6.6)

where
R
is the
R
-matrix and
\Phi,\Psi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
.

The minimal closed ribbon (op)curves are the face loops that encircle a face of
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
.
Since these loops depend on a base point, i.e., a site
s(a)
, we parametrize
them with the arrow
a\in\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)
. The opcurve versions of them are

\displaystyle\text{type 0 loop around }\mathcal{O}_{0}(a)\colon\quad\alpha_{a}=\bigl((T_{0}a)_{0}^{+},\bigl(T_{0}^{2}a\bigr)_{0}^{+},\dots,(T_{0}^{m}a)_{0}^{+}\bigr),

(6.7)

\displaystyle\text{type 1 loop around }\mathcal{O}_{1}(a)\colon\quad\beta_{a}=\bigl(a_{0}^{+},a_{2}^{+},(T_{1}a)_{0}^{+},(T_{1}a)_{2}^{+}\bigr),

(6.8)

\displaystyle\text{type 2 loop around }\mathcal{O}_{2}(a)\colon\quad\gamma_{a}=\bigl(a_{2}^{+},(T_{2}a)_{2}^{+},\dots,\bigl(T_{2}^{n-1}a\bigr)_{2}^{+}\bigr),

(6.9)

where
m=|\mathcal{O}_{0}(a)|
and
n=|\mathcal{O}_{2}(a)|
. The type 0 and type 2 loops are oriented counterclockwise and the type 1 loop clockwise, just like in
Figure
4
. The
\alpha_{a}
and
\gamma_{a}
are proper ribbons but
\beta_{a}
is maximally improper. In order to facilitate the computation of the opholonomies
of these opcurves we rewrite the formula (
6.3
) as follows:

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{i}^{\sigma})}=\theta_{a}\,\circ\,\pi_{i}\,\circ\,S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}^{(1-\sigma)/2},

where

\pi_{0},\pi_{2}\colon\ \mathcal{D}(H)^{*}\to\mathcal{D}(H)^{*},\qquad\pi_{0}(h\otimes\varphi):=h\otimes\varepsilon\cdot\varphi(1),\qquad\pi_{2}(h\otimes\varphi):=\varepsilon(h)\cdot 1\otimes\varphi,

are algebra endomorphisms of the dual of the double and the

\theta_{a}\colon\ H\otimes H^{*}\to\mathcal{M}(\Sigma),\qquad\theta_{a}(h\otimes\varphi):=P_{a}(h)Q_{a}(\varphi)

are linear injections.

Lemma 6.3
.

The maps
\theta_{a}
,
a\in\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)
, satisfy the following properties:

(i)

\theta_{a}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{0}^{+},a_{2}^{+})}
.

(ii)

\theta_{T_{1}a}=S_{\mathcal{M}}\,\circ\,\theta_{a}
.

(iii)

\theta_{a}(\Phi)\theta_{b}(\Psi)=\theta_{b}(\Psi)\theta_{a}(\Phi)
for all
\Phi,\Psi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
if
\mathcal{O}_{1}(a)\neq\mathcal{O}_{1}(b)
.

(iv)

The image of
\theta_{a}
is the edge algebra
\mathcal{M}_{e}\cong HH^{*}
on which
S_{\mathcal{M}}
acts as the

\mathcal{L}^{-1}\,\circ\,\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}^{-1}\,\circ\,\mathcal{L}
of (
3.12
) and (
3.13
).
The
\theta_{a}
identifies this action of
S_{\mathcal{M}}
with the action of the antipode of
\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
via

S_{\mathcal{M}}\,\circ\,\theta_{a}=\theta_{a}\,\circ\,S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}.

(v)

The
\theta_{a}
becomes an antialgebra map

\theta_{a}(\Phi)\theta_{a}(\Psi)=\theta_{a}(\Psi\underset{R}{\bullet}\Phi)

if we endow
\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
, as a monoid in the braided monoidal category of right
\mathcal{D}(H)
-modules, with the braided-opposite multiplication

\Psi\underset{R}{\bullet}\Phi:=(\Phi\leftharpoonup R_{1})(\Psi\leftharpoonup R_{2}).

Proof.

(i) Applying (
6.3
) and using the expression (
3.2
) for the coproduct, it is easy to see that the equation

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{0}^{+},a_{2}^{+})}(h\otimes\varphi)=P_{a}(h)Q_{a}(\varphi)
holds.

(ii) is essentially the definition of
S_{\mathcal{M}}
.

(iii) holds because
\mathcal{M}
is the tensor product of the edge algebras
\mathcal{M}_{e}
for
e\in\Sigma^{1}
.

(iv) can be seen by comparing formulas (
3.12
), (
3.13
) with the antipode formula (
3.3
).

(v) The braiding on
(g\otimes\psi)\otimes(h\otimes\varphi)\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}\otimes\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
acts by

\displaystyle[(h\otimes\varphi)\leftharpoonup R_{1}]\otimes[(g\otimes\psi)\leftharpoonup R_{2}]=\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\varepsilon\otimes x_{i},h_{(1)}\otimes\xi_{j}\varphi_{(1)}\xi_{k}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}\bigl(S^{-1}(x_{k})h_{(2)}x_{j}\otimes\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hphantom{[(h\otimes\varphi)\leftharpoonup R_1]\otimes[(g\otimes\psi)\leftharpoonup R_2]=}{}\otimes\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\xi_{i}\otimes 1,g_{(1)}\otimes\xi_{l}\psi_{(1)}\xi_{m}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}\bigl(S^{-1}(x_{m})g_{(2)}x_{l}\otimes\psi_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hphantom{[(h\otimes\varphi)\leftharpoonup R_1]\otimes[(g\otimes\psi)\leftharpoonup R_2]}{}=\bigl(S^{-1}(x_{k})hx_{j}\otimes\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)\otimes\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\xi_{j}\varphi_{(1)}\xi_{k},g_{(1)}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}\bigl(g_{(2)}\otimes\psi\bigr)

\displaystyle\hphantom{[(h\otimes\varphi)\leftharpoonup R_1]\otimes[(g\otimes\psi)\leftharpoonup R_2]}{}=\bigl(S^{-1}\bigl(g_{(3)}\bigr)hg_{(1)}\otimes\bigl(\varphi\leftharpoonup g_{(2)}\bigr)\bigr)\otimes\bigl(g_{(4)}\otimes\psi\bigr),

therefore

\displaystyle(g\otimes\psi)\underset{R}{\bullet}(h\otimes\varphi)=g_{(4)}S^{-1}\bigl(g_{(3)}\bigr)hg_{(1)}\otimes\bigl(\varphi\leftharpoonup g_{(2)}\bigr)\psi=h\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\rightharpoonup g\bigr)\otimes\varphi_{(2)}\psi

(even for non-involutive
H
-s) is precisely the multiplication of the smash product applied to
(h\otimes\varphi)\otimes(g\otimes\psi)\in(HH^{*})\otimes(HH^{*})
.
∎

Part (v) of the lemma immediately implies (cf.
[
15
, Theorem 7.6]
).

Corollary 6.4
.

The algebra
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{op}}
is isomorphic to the tensor product
\bigotimes_{e\in\Sigma^{1}}\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}_{R}
, where the algebra
\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}_{R}
is
\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
endowed with the
braided opposite multiplication.

Proposition 6.5
.

The opholonomy of the type
0
and
2
face loops
\alpha_{a}
and
\gamma_{a}
of (
6.7
) and (
6.9
) essentially reproduce the Gauss’ law and
flux operators of (
3.21
) and (
3.22
), respectively,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\alpha_{a}}(h\otimes\varphi)=\varphi(1)\cdot G_{a}(h),\qquad\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma_{a}}(h\otimes\varphi)=\varepsilon(h)\cdot F_{a}(\varphi).

The opholonomy of a type
1
loop is

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\beta_{a}}(h\otimes\varphi)=\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{M}}\cdot\bm{\langle}R_{1}S_{\mathcal{D}}(R_{2}),h\otimes\varphi\bm{\rangle},

(6.10)

where
R_{1}S_{\mathcal{D}}(R_{2})=S_{\mathcal{D}}(u)
with
u
denoting the Drinfeld element
S_{\mathcal{D}}(R_{2})R_{1}
of the quasitriangular Hopf algebra
\bm{\langle}\mathcal{D}(H),R\bm{\rangle}
.

Proof.

Iterating the formula
(\pi_{0}\otimes\operatorname{id})\,\circ\,\Delta_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}(h\otimes\varphi)=\bigl(h_{(1)}\otimes\varepsilon\bigr)\otimes\bigl(h_{(2)}\otimes\varphi\bigr)
for multiple coproducts we arrive to

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\alpha_{a}}(h\otimes\varphi)

\displaystyle{}=\theta_{T_{0}a}\bigl(h_{(1)}\otimes\varepsilon\bigr)\cdots\theta_{T_{0}^{m}a}\bigl(h_{(m)}\otimes\varepsilon\bigr)\cdot\varphi(1)

\displaystyle{}=P_{T_{0}a}\bigl(h_{(1)}\bigr)\cdots P_{T_{0}^{m}a}\bigl(h_{(m)}\bigr)\cdot\varphi(1)=G_{a}(h)\cdot\varphi(1).

Similarly, formula
(\operatorname{id}\otimes\pi_{2})\,\circ\,\Delta_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}(h\otimes\varphi)=\bigl(h\otimes\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)\otimes\bigl(1\otimes\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)
leads to

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma_{a}}(h\otimes\varphi)

\displaystyle{}=\varepsilon(h)\cdot\theta_{a}\bigl(1\otimes\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)\cdots\theta_{T_{2}^{n-1}a}\bigl(1\otimes\varphi_{(n)}\bigr)

\displaystyle{}=\varepsilon(h)\cdot Q_{a}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)\cdots Q_{T_{2}^{n-1}a}\bigl(\varphi_{(n)}\bigr)=\varepsilon(h)\cdot F_{a}(\varphi).

Using (i), (ii) and (iv) of Lemma
6.3
, the opholonomy of
\beta_{a}
can be written as

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\beta_{a}}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{0}^{+},a_{2}^{+})}\ast\bigl(S_{\mathcal{M}}\,\circ\,\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{0}^{+},a_{2}^{+})}\bigr)=\theta_{a}\ast(\theta_{a}\,\circ\,S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}),

which, by (v) of the lemma, evaluates to

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\beta_{a}}(h\otimes\varphi)=\theta_{a}\bigl(\bigl((h\otimes\varphi)_{(1)}\leftharpoonup R_{1}\bigr)\bigl(S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}\bigl((h\otimes\varphi)_{(2)}\bigr)\leftharpoonup R_{2}\bigr)\bigr)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\operatorname{Hol}^\circ_{\beta_a}(h\otimes\varphi)}{}=\theta_{a}\bigl((h\otimes\varphi)_{(2)}S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}\bigl((h\otimes\varphi)_{(3)}\bigr)\bigr)\bigl\bm{}{\langle}(h\otimes\varphi)_{(1)},R_{1}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}\bigl\bm{}{\langle}S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}\bigl((h\otimes\varphi)_{(4)}\bigr),R_{2}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\operatorname{Hol}^\circ_{\beta_a}(h\otimes\varphi)}{}=\theta_{a}(1_{\mathcal{D}^{*}})\cdot\bm{\langle}h\otimes\varphi,R_{1}S_{\mathcal{D}}(R_{2})\bm{\rangle}.

We note that
R_{1}S_{\mathcal{D}}(R_{2})=(\varepsilon\otimes x_{i})(S(\xi_{i})\otimes 1)=(\varepsilon\otimes S(x_{i}))(\xi_{i}\otimes 1)=S_{\mathcal{D}}(R_{1})R_{2}=S_{\mathcal{D}}(u)
.
∎

Since the only non-commuting
P
and
Q
operators are lying at corners of type 1 loops, the result (
6.10
) can be thought of as a holonomy
version of the Heisenberg exchange relations (
3.7
).

Using Proposition
6.2
(i), the above results can be translated to holonomies of the basic loop curves
\alpha_{a}^{\mathrm{rev}-1}
,
\beta_{a}^{\mathrm{rev}-1}

and
\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{rev}-1}
as follows:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}_{\alpha_{a}^{\mathrm{rev}-1}}(h\otimes\varepsilon)=S_{\mathcal{M}}(G_{a}(h))=P_{T_{1}T_{0}a}\bigl(h_{(1)}\bigr)P_{T_{1}T_{0}^{2}a}\bigl(h_{(2)}\bigr)\cdots P_{T_{1}T_{0}^{m}a}\bigl(h_{(m)}\bigr),

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}_{\beta_{a}^{\mathrm{rev}-1}}(h\otimes\varphi)=\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{M}}\cdot\bm{\langle}R_{1}S_{\mathcal{D}}(R_{2}),h\otimes\varphi\bm{\rangle},

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}_{\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{rev}-1}}(1\otimes\varphi)=S_{\mathcal{M}}(F_{a}(\varphi))=Q_{T_{1}a}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)Q_{T_{1}T_{2}a}\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)\cdots Q_{T_{1}T_{2}^{n-1}a}\bigl(\varphi_{(n)}\bigr).

The curve
\alpha_{a}^{\mathrm{rev}-1}
, if viewed on
\Sigma
, consists of arrows pointing toward the vertex
v=\mathcal{O}_{0}(a)
and, as a
\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\sim$}}{\mathrm{P}}
-curve, winds around
v

in clockwise direction. The curve
\gamma_{a}^{\mathrm{rev}-1}
consists of arrows on the boundary of the face
f=\mathcal{O}_{2}(a)
directed in clockwise direction.
These basic loops and their holonomies fit to the conventions used in the literature
[
3
,
15
]
. Although the automorphism
S_{\mathcal{M}}
provides a direct connection to our conventions, the simplicity of (
3.21
) and (
3.22
) can be an excuse for our preference of opholonomies.

The type 1 loops
\beta_{a}
and
\beta_{a}^{\mathrm{rev}-1}
belong to the following type of (op)curves:

Definition 6.6
.

A closed (op)curve
\zeta
on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
is called a central (op)curve if its (op)holonomy is of the form

(h\otimes\varphi)\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{M}}\cdot\bm{\langle}Z,h\otimes\varphi\bm{\rangle}
for some central element
Z
of
\mathcal{D}(H)
.

Notice that by convolution invertibility of (op)holonomies the
Z
in the above definition is necessarily invertible.
Due to Proposition
6.2
(i), a proper ribbon opcurve
\zeta
is central if and only if the proper ribbon curve
\zeta^{\mathrm{rev}}
is central.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
s\in\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*\,0}
be a site.

({\rm i})

The central opcurves
\zeta\colon s\to s
form a monoid under concatenation and their paths form a group.

({\rm ii})

If
\zeta\colon s\to s
is a central opcurve and
\alpha\colon s\to s^{\prime}
is any opcurve, then
\zeta^{\prime}:=\alpha^{-1}\zeta\alpha\colon s^{\prime}\to s^{\prime}
is a central opcurve, too.
Moreover,
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\zeta^{\prime}}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\zeta}
.

Proof.

(i) If
\zeta_{j}\colon s\to s
have
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\zeta_{j}}(\Psi)=\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{M}}\cdot\bm{\langle}Z_{j},\Psi\bm{\rangle}
for
j=1,2
, then

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\zeta_{1}\zeta_{2}}(\Psi)=\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{M}}\cdot\bm{\langle}Z_{1}Z_{2},\Psi\bm{\rangle}
. If
\zeta
is central, then so is
\zeta^{-1}
.

(ii) If we can prove the statement for
\alpha
having length 1, then the statement will follow by induction on the length of
\alpha
.
Let
\alpha=(d)
. Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(\Psi)

\displaystyle{}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d)}\bigl(S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}\bigl(\Psi_{(1)}\bigr)\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\zeta}\bigl(\Psi_{(2)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d)}\bigl(\Psi_{(3)}\bigr)

\displaystyle{}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d)}\bigl(S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}\bigl(\Psi_{(1)}\bigr)\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d)}\bigl(\Psi_{(3)}\bigr)\cdot\bigl\bm{}{\langle}Z,\Psi_{(2)}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}.

Evaluating
S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}\bigl(\Psi_{(1)}\bigr)\otimes\bm{\langle}Z,\Psi_{(2)}\bm{\rangle}\Psi_{(3)}
on
Y\otimes Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}(H)\otimes\mathcal{D}(H)
, one obtains

\bm{\langle}\Psi,S_{\mathcal{D}}(Y)ZY^{\prime}\bm{\rangle}=\bm{\langle}\Psi,S_{\mathcal{D}}(Y)Y^{\prime}Z\bm{\rangle}
, therefore

S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}\bigl(\Psi_{(1)}\bigr)\otimes\bigl\bm{}{\langle}Z,\Psi_{(2)}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}\Psi_{(3)}=S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}\bigl(\Psi_{(1)}\bigr)\otimes\Psi_{(2)}\bigl\bm{}{\langle}Z,\Psi_{(3)}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}

and

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(\Psi)

\displaystyle{}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d)}\bigl(S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}\bigl(\Psi_{(1)}\bigr)\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d)}\bigl(\Psi_{(2)}\bigr)\cdot\bigl\bm{}{\langle}Z,\Psi_{(3)}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}

\displaystyle{}=\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{M}}\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}\bigl(\Psi_{(1)}\bigr)\bigl\bm{}{\langle}Z,\Psi_{(2)}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}=\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{M}}\cdot\bm{\langle}Z,\Psi\bm{\rangle}

\displaystyle{}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\zeta}(\Psi).

∎

Definition 6.8
.

Let
\gamma\colon s\to s^{\prime}
be an opcurve on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
. A central deformation of
\gamma
is an opcurve
\delta\colon s\to s^{\prime}
such that
\delta\gamma^{-1}

is central.

7
The algebra of ribbon operators

In this section, we shall work with ribbon operators
M=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Psi)
for some ribbon path
\rho
and some
\Psi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
.
Ribbon operators can be multiplied either using the convolution product or the
multiplication of
\mathcal{M}
. The relations of the former type come from the definition of opholonomy,

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\alpha}\ast\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\beta}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\alpha\,\beta},\qquad\alpha,\beta\in\mathsf{Ribb}_{L}^{\circ}\quad\text{or}\quad\alpha,\beta\in\mathsf{Ribb}_{R}^{\circ}.

So we can, for example, change the starting point of the Gauss’ law and flux operators by conjugation in the convolution sense,

\displaystyle G_{T_{0}a}(h)=P_{T_{0}a}(S\bigl(h_{(1)}\bigr))G_{a}\bigl(h_{(2)}\bigr)P_{T_{0}a}\bigl(h_{(3)}\bigr),

(7.1)

\displaystyle F_{T_{2}a}(\varphi)=Q_{a}(S\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\bigr))F_{a}\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)Q_{a}\bigl(\varphi_{(3)}\bigr).

(7.2)

As for the ordinary multiplication of
\mathcal{M}
, it is quite difficult to find computable relations between ribbon operators.
The rare cases which we are able to compute occupy the present Section.

If two ribbons are sufficiently distant, then the corresponding operators should commute. Thinking over which edges have
P
or
Q
operators that do not
commute we arrive to the following:

Definition 7.1
.

For an arrow
d\in\mathrm{Arr}(\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*})
, let
U(d)
be the set of arrows that are incident to
d
and lie on the boundary of the same type 1 face
as
d
does. (There are 6 such arrows, including
d
, forming a U-shape of 3 edges.) Two (op)curves, or just sets of arrows,
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}

in
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
are called U-separated, written
\gamma\underset{\text{U}}{\leftrightarrow}\gamma^{\prime}
if
d\not\in U(d^{\prime})
for all
d\in\gamma
and
d^{\prime}\in\gamma^{\prime}
.

So we have

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma}(\Psi)=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma}(\Psi)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)\qquad\forall\Phi,\Psi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}\quad\text{if}\ \rho\underset{\text{U}}{\leftrightarrow}\gamma.

(7.3)

At the other extreme let
\rho=\gamma
. Then the product is computable if
d\not\in U(d^{\prime})
for any pair of different arrows
d,d^{\prime}\in\rho
. This property
of being “self-U-separated” can be seen to be equivalent to properness (Definition
6.1
) of the opcurve. Thus,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Psi)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi\Psi)\qquad\text{if}\ \rho\in\mathsf{Ribb}^{\circ}_{L}\ \text{is proper}

(7.4)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Psi\Phi)\qquad\text{if}\ \rho\in\mathsf{Ribb}^{\circ}_{R}\ \text{is proper}

by comparing (
6.3
) with (
3.1
) and using that the coproduct of
\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
is multiplicative. For proper ribbons, we also have the inversion formula

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho^{-1}}(\Phi)=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}(\Phi))\qquad\text{if}\ \rho\ \text{is proper}

(7.5)

and the cyclic permutation formula

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{C\rho}(\Psi)=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Psi)\qquad\text{if}\ \rho\ \text{is closed, proper and}\ \Psi\in\operatorname{Cocom}\mathcal{D}(H)^{*},

(7.6)

where
C\rho=(d_{2},\dots,d_{n},d_{1})
whenever
\rho=(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})
.

Now consider the length 2 opcurves that are not straight, the “elbows”.
Up to taking inverses, there are 4 such opcurves with common middle point being the site
s(a)
:

\bigl(a_{0}^{+},a_{2}^{+}\bigr),\qquad\bigl((T_{2}^{-1}a)_{2}^{+},a_{2}^{+}\bigr),\qquad\bigl((T_{2}^{-1}a)_{2}^{+},(T_{0}a)_{0}^{+}\bigr),\qquad\bigl(a_{0}^{+},(T_{0}a)_{0}^{+}\bigr).

The following lemma is known in the context of Hopf algebra gauge theory
[
15
]
.

Lemma 7.2
.

The opholonomies of an “opposite pair of elbows” commute. That is to say,

\displaystyle\big[\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{0}^{+},a_{2}^{+})}(\Phi),\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{((T_{2}^{-1}a)_{2}^{+},(T_{0}a)_{0}^{+})}(\Psi)\big]=0,

(7.7)

\displaystyle\big[\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{((T_{2}^{-1}a)_{2}^{+},a_{2}^{+})}(\Phi),\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{0}^{+},(T_{0}a)_{0}^{+})}(\Psi)\big]=0

(7.8)

for all
\Phi,\Psi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
.
If we replace any one or both of the elbows in (
7.7
) or (
7.8
) by their inverses commutativity of their opholonomies
remains true.

Proof.

(
7.7
) follows from (
7.3
) since the two curves are U-separated.
(
7.8
) can be shown by checking that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{((T_{2}^{-1}a)_{2}^{+},a_{2}^{+})}(g\otimes\varphi)=\varepsilon(g)\cdot Q_{T_{2}^{-1}a}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)Q_{a}\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\bigr),

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{0}^{+},(T_{0}a)_{0}^{+})}(h\otimes\psi)=\psi(1)\cdot P_{a}\bigl(h_{(1)}\bigr)P_{T_{0}a}\bigl(h_{(2)}\bigr)

and then computing

\displaystyle P_{a}\bigl(h_{(1)}\bigr)P_{T_{0}a}\bigl(h_{(2)}\bigr)\cdot Q_{T_{2}^{-1}a}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)Q_{a}\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)=

\displaystyle\quad\ {}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{QPbar inv-comm rel})}}{{=}}P_{a}\bigl(h_{(1)}\bigr)Q_{T_{2}^{-1}a}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)P_{T_{0}a}\bigl(h_{(2)}\leftharpoonup\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)Q_{a}\bigl(\varphi_{(3)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=Q_{T_{2}^{-1}a}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)P_{a}\bigl(h_{(1)}\bigr)Q_{a}\bigl(\varphi_{(3)}\bigr)P_{T_{0}a}\bigl(h_{(2)}\leftharpoonup\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad\ {}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{QP inv-comm rel})}}{{=}}Q_{T_{2}^{-1}a}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)Q_{a}\bigl(\varphi_{(4)}\bigr)P_{a}(S\bigl(\varphi_{(3)}\bigr)\rightharpoonup h_{(1)})P_{T_{0}a}\bigl(h_{(2)}\leftharpoonup\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=Q_{T_{2}^{-1}a}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)Q_{a}\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)\cdot P_{a}\bigl(h_{(1)}\bigr)P_{T_{0}a}\bigl(h_{(2)}\bigr).

Since the elbows in (
7.8
) are proper ribbons, replacing any one of them with its inverse amounts only to applying
S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}
on
\Phi

or
\Psi
, see (
7.5
).
Taking inverses of the elbows of (
7.7
) requires more work,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{0}^{+},a_{2}^{+})^{-1}}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{((T_{1}a)_{0}^{+},(T_{1}a)_{2}^{+})}\ast\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\beta_{a}^{-1}},

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{((T_{2}^{-1}a)_{2}^{+},(T_{0}a)_{0}^{+})^{-1}}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{((T_{1}T_{2}^{-1}a)_{2}^{+},(T_{1}T_{0}a)_{0}^{+})}\ast\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{{\beta^{\prime}}_{b}^{-1}},

where
b=T_{2}^{-1}a
and
\beta^{\prime}_{b}:=\bigl(b_{0}^{-}\bigr)\beta_{b}\bigl(b_{0}^{+}\bigr)
. The appearance of
T_{1}
in the first terms does not change U-separatedness of the curves and
the
\beta_{a}
and
\beta^{\prime}_{b}
being central the second terms do not change commutativity, either.
∎

Corollary 7.3
.

Let
\lambda
be either a type
0
or a type
2
face loop. Let
\epsilon=(d,b)
be an elbow opcurve with midpoint
\nabla_{1}d=\nabla_{0}b=s
which intersects

\lambda
only at the site
s
. If
s
is not the base point of
\lambda
, then

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\epsilon}(\Phi)
commutes with
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\lambda}(\Psi)
for every
\Phi,\Psi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
.

The above corollary is the first instance of a more general commutativity theorem of holonomies of type 0 or type 2 face loops with certain ribbon
operators; expressing a sort of “gauge invariance” of the ribbon operator. Before formulating the statement, let us recall some Hopf algebra.

If
h\in\operatorname{Cocom}H
, then the iterated coproducts
h_{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes h_{(n)}
are invariant under cyclic permutations.
In particular, the Gauss’ law operators
G_{a}(h)
are independent of the choice of the base point,
G_{T_{0}a}(h)=G_{a}(h)
, if
h\in\operatorname{Cocom}H
.
The same holds for the flux operators
F_{a}(\varphi)
if
\varphi\in\operatorname{Cocom}H^{*}
. A cocommutative element
\varphi
defines a trace on
H
by the
evaluation,
{\bm{\langle}\varphi,hg\bm{\rangle}=\bm{\langle}\varphi,gh\bm{\rangle}}
. All traces on
H
are of this form. Since the Haar integral
\iota
is cocommutative and
the map
H\to H^{*}
,
h\mapsto\iota\leftharpoonup h
is an isomorphism
[
20
]
, every
\varphi\in\operatorname{Cocom}H^{*}
is of the form
\varphi=\iota\leftharpoonup z

for a unique central element
z
of
H
.

The relation of cocommutative elements of
H
and
H^{*}
with that of
\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
is intricate. Suppose we want the holonomy of the type 0 face loop
\alpha_{a}

to be independent of the base point. For which elements
\Psi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
do we have the relation
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\alpha_{T_{0}a}}(\Psi)=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\alpha_{a}}(\Psi)
?
One answer is provided by (
7.6
) saying that
\Psi\in\operatorname{Cocom}\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
. Another answer is obtained by Proposition
6.5

and by equation (
7.1
) saying that
\Psi=h\otimes\varepsilon
with
h\in\operatorname{Cocom}H
. Which answer implies the other? None of them. The point is that the surjective
coalgebra maps

H\quad\overset{\operatorname{id}\otimes\varepsilon}{\parbox{40.0pt}{\begin{picture}\put(37.0,4.0){\vector{-1}{0}{34.0}}\end{picture}}}\quad\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}\quad\overset{\varepsilon\otimes\operatorname{id}}{\parbox{40.0pt}{\begin{picture}\put(3.0,4.0){\vector{1}{0}{34.0}}\end{picture}}}\quad H^{*}

if restricted to
\operatorname{Cocom}\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
become mere linear maps

\operatorname{Cocom}H\quad\overset{\operatorname{id}\otimes\varepsilon}{\parbox{40.0pt}{\begin{picture}\put(37.0,4.0){\vector{-1}{0}{34.0}}\end{picture}}}\quad\operatorname{Cocom}\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}\quad\overset{\varepsilon\otimes\operatorname{id}}{\parbox{40.0pt}{\begin{picture}\put(3.0,4.0){\vector{1}{0}{34.0}}\end{picture}}}\quad\operatorname{Cocom}H^{*},

which are no longer surjective, unless
\operatorname{Cocom}H
, resp.
\operatorname{Cocom}H^{*}
, is commutative. This is why condition A of the theorem below contains a list of 3 possibilities.

Theorem 7.4
.

Let
\rho\colon s_{0}\to s_{1}
be a ribbon opcurve
(
Definition
\ref{def: ribbon})
and let
\lambda
be either a type
0
face loop or a type
2
face loop which
is disjoint from both
s_{0}
and
s_{1}
. Then

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)\ \text{and }\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\lambda}(\Psi)\ \text{commute for all }\Phi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}

provided one of the following conditions holds:

A.

\Psi
belongs to either
\operatorname{Cocom}\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
or
\operatorname{Cocom}H\otimes\varepsilon
or
1\otimes\operatorname{Cocom}H^{*}
.

B.

\Psi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
is arbitrary but the base point of
\lambda
is not lying on
\rho
.

Proof.

First we assume that
\rho
is a left ribbon and
\lambda
is a type 2 face loop. Let
w_{\rho}\in\bigl\{T_{0}^{-1},T_{2}\bigr\}^{*}
be the code word of
\rho

(Definition
5.7
).
Let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}
be the list of generalized subcurves of
\rho
such that (1) all arrows and sites of
\alpha_{j}
belongs to
\lambda
and
(2)
\alpha_{j}
is maximal among the subcurves satisfying (1). Mentioning sites under (1) refers to the possibility that
\alpha_{j}
consists of a single
site. It is maximal if and only if none of its neighbour arrows in
\rho
belongs to
\lambda
.
The arrow
d_{j}^{\mathrm{in}}
of
\rho
preceding
\alpha_{j}
and the arrow
d_{j}^{\mathrm{out}}
succeeding it must belong to
U(\lambda)
but not to
\lambda
. These arrows
are uniquely determined by the arc
\alpha_{j}
as follows. If
\alpha_{j}=(s)
is trivial, then
\bigl(d_{j}^{\mathrm{in}},d_{j}^{\mathrm{out}}\bigr)
is the unique elbow which touches

\lambda
only at the site
s
. If
\alpha_{j}=\bigl(c_{j,1},\dots,c_{j,l_{j}}\bigr)
is non-trivial, then the
\bigl(d_{j}^{\mathrm{in}},c_{j,1}\bigr)
and
\bigl(c_{j,l_{j}},d_{j}^{\mathrm{out}}\bigr)

are straight (do not turn either left or right). The existence of such
d_{j}^{\mathrm{in}/\mathrm{out}}
in
\rho
for all
j
follows either from maximality of
the arcs
\alpha_{j}
or
(
for
d_{1}^{\mathrm{in}}
and
d_{k}^{\mathrm{out}}
)
from the assumption that
s_{0}
,
s_{1}
do not lie on
\lambda
. Let
\gamma_{j}:=\bigl(d_{j}^{\mathrm{in}}\bigr)\alpha_{j}\bigl(d_{j}^{\mathrm{out}}\bigr)
. Then every
\gamma_{j}
corresponds to a subword
T_{0}^{-1}T_{2}^{l_{j}}T_{0}^{-1}
of
w_{\rho}
and the whole ribbon can be written
as the composite

\rho=\delta_{0}\gamma_{1}\delta_{1}\cdots\gamma_{k}\delta_{k}

of possibly empty
1
1

1

\delta_{j}
is empty if
\bigl(d_{j}^{\mathrm{out}},d_{j+1}^{\mathrm{in}}\bigr)
is a type 0 loop of length 2. Such double edges in
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
correspond to
a 2-valent vertex in
\Sigma
.
curves
\delta_{j}
U-separated from
\lambda
and of “collars”
\gamma_{j}
of
\lambda
. These collars need not be
as simple as the one of Figure
5
. They can wind around
\lambda
arbitrary many times, although always counterclockwise. For different indices,
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{j}
may overlap arbitrarily or can even coincide.

Figure 5:
To the proof of Theorem
7.4
: A subcurve
\gamma
of a left ribbon which collars the face loop
\lambda
of type 2
and its central deformation
\gamma^{\prime}
.

It is now clear that commutativity of the opholonomies of
\rho
and
\lambda
will follow from that of
\gamma
and
\lambda
for a single collar

\gamma=\bigl(d^{\mathrm{in}},c_{1},\dots,c_{l},d^{\mathrm{out}}\bigr)
. A central deformation
\gamma^{\prime}
of
\gamma
can be defined in the code word representation
(Lemma
5.7
) by means of a homomorphism
\smash{\mathfrak{h}\colon\bigl\{T_{0}^{-1},T_{2}\bigr\}^{*}}\to\smash{\bigl\{T_{0},T_{0}^{-1},T_{2},T_{2}^{-1}\bigr\}^{*}}
of free monoids as

\gamma^{\prime}:=\bm{\langle}a_{0},\mathfrak{h}(w_{\gamma})\bm{\rangle},\qquad\text{where}\quad\mathfrak{h}\bigl(T_{0}^{-1}\bigr):=T_{0}^{-1},\ \mathfrak{h}(T_{2}):=T_{0}^{-1}T_{2}^{-1}T_{0}^{-1},

where
\gamma=\bm{\langle}a_{0},w_{\gamma}\bm{\rangle}
. This means that
\gamma^{\prime}
differs from
\gamma
by replacing each
c_{j}
of color
T_{2}
by a detour

\bigl(b_{j},c^{\prime}_{j},d_{j}\bigr)
of color
\mathfrak{h}(T_{2})
such that the difference
T_{2}\mathfrak{h}(T_{2})^{-1}=T_{2}T_{0}T_{2}T_{0}
is a type 1 loop, a conjugate of some

\beta_{a}
, hence a central curve. To see that
\gamma^{\prime}
is a central deformation of
\gamma
introduce the opcurves
\sigma_{j}:=\bigl(d^{\mathrm{in}},c_{1},\dots,c_{j}\bigr)

and the lassos
\vartheta_{j}:=\sigma_{j}\beta_{j}\sigma_{j}^{-1}
with the central curve
\beta_{j}=\bigl(\tilde{\mathbb{T}}_{1}c_{j},b_{j},c^{\prime}_{j},d_{j}\bigr)^{-1}
. Then we have equality of paths

\gamma{\gamma^{\prime}}^{-1}=\vartheta_{l}\cdots\vartheta_{1}

proving that
\gamma{\gamma^{\prime}}^{-1}
is a central opcurve by Lemma
6.7
. Thus,

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma{\gamma^{\prime}}^{-1}}\ast\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma^{\prime}}=\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{M}}\cdot\bm{\langle}Z,\mbox{\,\rm\_}\,\bm{\rangle}\ast\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma^{\prime}}

(for some
Z\in\operatorname{Center}(\mathcal{D}(H))
) commutes with
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\lambda}(\Psi)
if and only if
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma^{\prime}}
does. We write
\gamma^{\prime}
as the composite

(d_{0},b_{1})(c^{\prime}_{1})(d_{1},b_{2})(c^{\prime}_{2})\cdots(c^{\prime}_{l})(d_{l},b_{l+1}),\qquad\text{where}\quad d_{0}=d^{\mathrm{in}},\quad b_{l+1}=d^{\mathrm{out}}

of elbows
\bigl(d_{j},b_{j+1}\bigr)
touching
\lambda
at a single site and of length 1 pieces
\bigl(c^{\prime}_{j}\bigr)
. The elbows commute with
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\lambda}(\Psi)

because – in case A – the assumption on
\Psi
ensures
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\lambda}(\Psi)
is independent of the base point of
\lambda
so that it can be
shifted to become different from the contact point of the elbow and Corollary
7.3
to apply – and in case B – the base point of

\lambda
is different from the contact point and Corollary
7.3
directly applies.
We claim that
\bigl(c^{\prime}_{j}\bigr)
is U-separated from
\lambda
. Assume it is not. Then at least one endpoint of
c^{\prime}_{j}
lies
on
\lambda
so either
b_{j}
or
d_{j}
connects 2 sites of
\lambda
. Let
s(a_{1})
and
s(a_{2})
be these two sites. Since they lie on the face loop

\lambda
,
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
must belong to the same
T_{2}
-orbit on
\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)
. But
s(a_{1})
and
s(a_{2})
are also connected by
b_{j}
or
d_{j}
which
have color
T_{0}^{-1}
, so
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
belong to the same
T_{0}
-orbit. By Lemma
2.2
(iii), this is possible only if
a_{1}=a_{2}
so
b_{j}

or
d_{j}
is a loop which cannot exist on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
. This contradiction refutes the assumption that
\bigl(c^{\prime}_{j}\bigr)
is not U-separated from
\lambda
.
Hence,
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\lambda}(\Psi)
commutes also with the
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(c^{\prime}_{j})}(\Phi)
for all
j
and finally also with
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma^{\prime}}
.
This finishes the proof of the theorem for left ribbons
\rho
and type 2 face loops
\lambda
.

If
\rho
is a left ribbon and
\lambda
is a type 0 face loop, then the proof goes exactly as above except that the collars
\gamma
now, having color code

T_{2}\bigl(T_{0}^{-1}\bigr)^{l}T_{2}
, wind around
\lambda
in clockwise direction. The deformation
\gamma^{\prime}
is given by the substitution

T_{0}^{-1}\mapsto T_{2}T_{0}T_{2}
,
T_{2}\mapsto T_{2}
.

If
\rho
is a right ribbon, then the collars go clockwise around type 2 face loops and counterclockwise around type 0 loops.
Otherwise, the proof is the same.
∎

Corollary 7.5
.

For every ribbon
\rho\colon\bm{\langle}v_{0},f_{0}\bm{\rangle}\to\bm{\langle}v_{1},f_{1}\bm{\rangle}
and for every
\Phi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
, the operator
M=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)
commutes with all the
operators
A_{v}
and
B_{f}
except for
v\in\{v_{0},v_{1}\}
and
{f\in\{f_{0},f_{1}\}}
.

For a given ribbon, there are four type 0 or type 2 face loops for which the above theorem does not apply.
The next theorem is to deal with these remaining cases.

Theorem 7.6
.

Let
\rho\colon s(a)\to s(b)
be a proper left ribbon, where
s(a)
and
s(b)
are disjoint sites. Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)\underset{\scriptscriptstyle a}{\triangleleft}X=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi\leftharpoonup X),

(7.9)

\displaystyle X\underset{\scriptscriptstyle b}{\triangleright}\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(X\rightharpoonup\Phi)

(7.10)

hold true for every
X\in\mathcal{D}(H)
and
\Phi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
.

Proof.

The equations to be proven are equivalent to the exchange relations

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)\mathbf{D}_{a}(X)=\mathbf{D}_{a}\bigl(X_{(1)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}\bigl(\Phi\leftharpoonup X_{(2)}\bigr),

(7.11)

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{b}(X)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}\bigl(X_{(1)}\rightharpoonup\Phi\bigr)\mathbf{D}_{b}\bigl(X_{(2)}\bigr).

(7.12)

Every arrow of
\rho
is either a
c_{0}^{-}
or a
c_{2}^{+}
for some
c\in\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)
. The consecutive
c_{0}^{-}
arrows form a non-trivial arc on a type 0 face loop
and the consecutive
c_{2}^{+}
arrows do the same on a type 2 face loop. So there is a unique decomposition
\rho=\upsilon_{1}\cdots\upsilon_{m}
into a concatenation
of arcs which are alternatingly type 0 or type 2.
(
This decomposition corresponds to the decomposition of a word
w\in\bigl\{T_{0}^{-1},T_{2}\bigr\}^{*}
into an alternating product
of powers
T_{0}^{-k}
and
T_{2}^{l}
.
)
Since
\rho
is proper, two arcs can intersect only at some of their common endpoints and in this case the two arcs are consecutive in the
sequence
(\upsilon_{1},\dots,\upsilon_{m})
. The number of arcs
m\geq 2
because we assumed that
s(a)
and
s(b)
are disjoint.

The face loops
\alpha_{a}
,
\gamma_{a}
and
\alpha_{b}
,
\gamma_{b}
involved in the gauge transformations
\mathbf{D}_{a}(X)
and
\mathbf{D}_{b}(X)
fail to commute only with a
few initial and final arcs. More precisely, let
\upsilon_{j}\colon s_{j-1}\to s_{j}
and assume that
\upsilon_{1}
is type 2. Then
s_{1}
does not lie on
\alpha_{a}
,
otherwise two different sites,
s_{0}
and
s_{1}
, belonged to both a type 0 and a type 2 face loop which is impossible in view of Figure
4
.
So neither endpoints of
\upsilon_{2}\cdots\upsilon_{m}
belongs to
\alpha_{a}
. Furthermore,
\upsilon_{2}\cdots\upsilon_{m}
never visits the site
s(a)=s_{0}
because
\upsilon_{1}
does
but
\rho
is proper, hence a simple curve by Proposition
6.2
(ii). It follows that

\upsilon_{1}\ \text{is type 2}\quad\Rightarrow\quad G_{a}(H)\ \text{commutes with}\ \operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{2}\dots\upsilon_{m}}(\mathcal{D}(H)^{*})

(7.13)

by Theorem
7.4
(B).
Now assume that
\upsilon_{1}
is type 0. Then
s_{1}
lies on
\alpha_{a}
and
s_{2}
does not because
\upsilon_{2}
is type 2. Therefore,

\upsilon_{1}\ \text{is type 0}\quad\Rightarrow\quad G_{a}(H)\ \text{commutes with}\ \operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{3}\dots\upsilon_{m}}(\mathcal{D}(H)^{*})

(7.14)

again by Theorem
7.4
(B). Similar arguments yield

\displaystyle\upsilon_{1}\ \text{is type 0}\quad\Rightarrow\quad F_{a}(H^{*})\ \text{commutes with}\ \operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{2}\dots\upsilon_{m}}(\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}),

(7.15)

\displaystyle\upsilon_{1}\ \text{is type 2}\quad\Rightarrow\quad F_{a}(H^{*})\ \text{commutes with}\ \operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{3}\dots\upsilon_{m}}(\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}).

(7.16)

Similar conclusions can be drawn for commutativity of
F_{b}(H^{*})
and
G_{b}(H)
with
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{1}\dots\upsilon_{m-1}}
or
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{1}\dots\upsilon_{m-2}}

but we shall not need them.

Concentrating on the relation (
7.11
) we have 6 different exchange relations to compute:

Case
\bm{G_{a}}
with
\bm{\upsilon_{1}}
if
\bm{\upsilon_{1}}
is type 0.
Let
n=|\mathcal{O}_{0}(a)|
. Then the arc is
\smash{\upsilon_{1}=\bigl(a_{0}^{-},\bigl(T_{0}^{-1}a\bigr)_{0}^{-},\dots,}\allowbreak\smash{\bigl(T_{0}^{-j+1}a\bigr)_{0}^{-}\bigr)}

for some
0<j<n
. Since
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{1}}(h\otimes\varphi)
is proportional to
\varphi(1)
, it suffices to work with

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{1}}(h\otimes\varepsilon)=P^{S}_{a,\dots,T_{0}^{-j+1}a}(h)
where we use the notation
P_{c_{1},\dots,c_{i}}(h):=P_{c_{1}}\bigl(h_{(1)}\bigr)\cdots P_{c_{i}}\bigl(h_{(i)}\bigr)
and similarly for

P^{S}_{c}=P_{c}\,\circ\,S
,

\displaystyle P^{S}_{a,\dots,T_{0}^{-j+1}a}(h)G_{a}(g)

\displaystyle{}=P_{T_{0}^{n-j+1}a,\dots,T_{0}^{n}a}(S(h))P_{T_{0}a,\dots,T_{0}^{n-j}a}\bigl(g_{(1)}\bigr)P_{T_{0}^{n-j+1}a,\dots,T_{0}^{n}a}\bigl(g_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle{}=P_{T_{0}a,\dots,T_{0}^{n-j}a}\bigl(g_{(1)}\bigr)P_{T_{0}^{n-j+1}a,\dots,T_{0}^{n}a}\bigl(S(h)g_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle{}=G_{a}\bigl(g_{(1)}\bigr)P_{T_{0}^{n-j+1}a,\dots,T_{0}^{n}a}\bigl(S\bigl(g_{(2)}\bigr)S(h)g_{(3)}\bigr)

\displaystyle{}=G_{a}\bigl(g_{(1)}\bigr)P^{S}_{a,\dots,T_{0}^{-j+1}a}\bigl(S\bigl(g_{(3)}\bigr)hg_{(2)}\bigr).

Case
\bm{G_{a}}
with
\bm{\upsilon_{2}}
if
\bm{\upsilon_{1}}
is type 0.
Given
\upsilon_{1}
as in the previous case the next arc
\upsilon_{2}
starts at
s\bigl(T_{0}^{-j}a\bigr)
therefore

\upsilon_{2}=\bigl(\bigl(T_{0}^{-j}a\bigr)_{2}^{+},\bigl(T_{2}T_{0}^{-j}a\bigr)_{2}^{+},\dots,\bigl(T_{2}^{k-1}T_{0}^{-j}a\bigr)_{2}^{+}\bigr)
for some
0<k<\!\big|\mathcal{O}_{2}\bigl(T_{0}^{-j}a\bigr)\big|
. Nontriviality of the commutator of

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{2}}(1\otimes\varphi)=Q_{T_{0}^{-j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{k-1}T_{0}^{-j}a}(\varphi)
and
G_{a}
comes from the first arrow of
\upsilon_{2}
and from the

(n-j)
-th arrow of
G_{a}
,

\displaystyle Q_{T_{0}^{-j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{k-1}T_{0}^{-j}a}(\varphi)G_{a}(g)=Q_{T_{0}^{-j}a}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)G_{a}(g)Q_{T_{2}T_{0}^{-j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{k-1}T_{0}^{-j}a}\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=P_{T_{0}a,\dots,T_{0}^{n-j-1}a}\bigl(g_{(1)}\bigr)Q_{T_{0}^{-j}a}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)P_{T_{0}^{n-j}a}\bigl(g_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}\times P_{T_{0}^{n-j+1}a,\dots,T_{0}^{n}a}\bigl(g_{(3)}\bigr)Q_{T_{2}T_{0}^{-j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{k-1}T_{0}^{-j}a}\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad\ \ {}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Bd cap Cb}}}{{=}}P_{T_{0}a,\dots,T_{0}^{n-j-1}a}\bigl(g_{(1)}\bigr)P_{T_{0}^{n-j}a}(\varphi_{(1)}\rightharpoonup g_{(2)})Q_{T_{0}^{-j}a}\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}\times P_{T_{0}^{n-j+1}a,\dots,T_{0}^{n}a}\bigl(g_{(3)}\bigr)Q_{T_{2}T_{0}^{-j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{k-1}T_{0}^{-j}a}\bigl(\varphi_{(3)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=P_{T_{0}a,\dots,T_{0}^{n-j}a}\bigl(g_{(1)}\bigr)P_{T_{0}^{n-j+1}a,\dots,T_{0}^{n}a}\bigl(g_{(3)}\bigr)Q_{T_{0}^{-j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{k-1}T_{0}^{-j}a}\bigl(\varphi\leftharpoonup g_{(2)}\bigr).

This is not yet in the desired form, there is no
G_{a}
on the left-hand side, but we wish to complete it in the presence of the holonomy of
\upsilon_{1}
:

\displaystyle P^{S}_{a,\dots,T_{0}^{-j+1}a}(h)Q_{T_{0}^{-j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{k-1}T_{0}^{-j}a}(\varphi)G_{a}(g)

\displaystyle=P_{T_{0}^{n-j+1}a,\dots,T_{0}^{n}a}(S(h))P_{T_{0}a,\dots,T_{0}^{n-j}a}\bigl(g_{(1)}\bigr)P_{T_{0}^{n-j+1}a,\dots,T_{0}^{n}a}\bigl(g_{(3)}\bigr)Q_{T_{0}^{-j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{k-1}T_{0}^{-j}a}\bigl(\varphi\leftharpoonup g_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle=P_{T_{0}a,\dots,T_{0}^{n-j}a}\bigl(g_{(1)}\bigr)P_{T_{0}^{n-j+1}a,\dots,T_{0}^{n}a}\bigl(S(h)g_{(3)}\bigr)Q_{T_{0}^{-j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{k-1}T_{0}^{-j}a}\bigl(\varphi\leftharpoonup g_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle=G_{a}\bigl(g_{(1)}\bigr)P_{T_{0}^{n-j+1}a,\dots,T_{0}^{n}a}\bigl(S\bigl(g_{(2)}\bigr)S(h)g_{(4)}\bigr)Q_{T_{0}^{-j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{k-1}T_{0}^{-j}a}\bigl(\varphi\leftharpoonup g_{(3)}\bigr)

\displaystyle=G_{a}\bigl(g_{(1)}\bigr)P^{S}_{a,\dots,T_{0}^{-j+1}a}\bigl(S\bigl(g_{(4)}\bigr)hg_{(2)}\bigr)Q_{T_{0}^{-j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{k-1}T_{0}^{-j}a}\bigl(\varphi\leftharpoonup g_{(3)}\bigr).

Since
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{1}\upsilon_{2}}(\Phi)=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{1}}(h\otimes\varepsilon)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{2}}(1\otimes\varphi)
, where
\Phi=h\otimes\varphi
and

\displaystyle S\bigl(g_{(3)}\bigr)hg_{(1)}\otimes\varphi\leftharpoonup g_{(2)}=\bigl\bm{}{\langle}h_{(1)}\otimes\xi_{i}\varphi_{(1)}\xi_{j},\varepsilon\otimes g\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}S(x_{j})h_{(2)}x_{i}\otimes\varphi_{(2)}=\Phi\leftharpoonup(\varepsilon\otimes g),

we obtain
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{1}\upsilon_{2}}(\Phi)G_{a}(g)=G_{a}\bigl(g_{(1)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{1}\upsilon_{2}}\bigl(\Phi\leftharpoonup\bigl(\varepsilon\otimes g_{(2)}\bigr)\bigr)
which, together with
(
7.14
), gives the result

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)G_{a}(g)=G_{a}\bigl(g_{(1)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}\bigl(\Phi\leftharpoonup\bigl(\varepsilon\otimes g_{(2)}\bigr)\bigr),

(7.17)

whenever
\upsilon_{1}
is a type 0 arc.

Case
\bm{F_{a}}
with
\bm{\upsilon_{1}}
if
\bm{\upsilon_{1}}
is type 0.

For
\upsilon_{1}
as above, we can write

\displaystyle P^{S}_{a,\dots,T_{0}^{-j+1}a}(h)F_{a}(\psi)=P_{a}\bigl(S\bigl(h_{(1)}\bigr)\bigr)F_{a}(\psi)P_{T_{0}^{-1}a}\bigl(S\bigl(h_{(2)}\bigr)\bigr)\cdots P_{T_{0}^{-j+1}a}\bigl(S\bigl(h_{(j)}\bigr)\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad\ {}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{rhoinv-lamb}}}{{=}}F_{a}\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\bigr)P_{a}\bigl(S\bigl(\psi_{(1)}\bigr)\rightharpoonup S\bigl(h_{(1)}\bigr)\bigr)P_{T_{0}^{-1}a}\bigl(S\bigl(h_{(2)}\bigr)\bigr)\cdots P_{T_{0}^{-j+1}a}\bigl(S\bigl(h_{(j)}\bigr)\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=F_{a}\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\bigr)P^{S}_{a,\dots,T_{0}^{-j+1}a}\bigl(h\leftharpoonup\psi_{(1)}\bigr).

Since

\displaystyle\Phi\leftharpoonup(\psi\otimes 1)=\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\psi,h_{(1)}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}h_{(2)}\otimes\varphi=h\leftharpoonup\psi\otimes\varphi,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{1}}(\Phi\leftharpoonup(\psi\otimes 1))=P^{S}_{a,\dots,T_{0}^{-j+1}a}(h\leftharpoonup\psi)\cdot\bm{\langle}\varphi,1\bm{\rangle},

using also (
7.15
), we immediately obtain

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)F_{a}(\psi)=F_{a}\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}\bigl(\Phi\leftharpoonup\bigl(\psi_{(1)}\otimes 1\bigr)\bigr),

(7.18)

which together with (
7.17
) proves (
7.11
) at least if the first arc of
\rho
is type 0.

Case
\bm{G_{a}}
with
\bm{\upsilon_{1}}
if
\bm{\upsilon_{1}}
is type 2.
Then
\upsilon_{1}=\bigl(a_{2}^{+},\dots,\bigl(T_{2}^{j-1}a\bigr)_{2}^{+}\bigr)
for some
0<j<n
where
n=|\mathcal{O}_{2}(a)|
. Thus,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{1}}(\Phi)G_{a}(g)={}

\displaystyle\varepsilon(h)Q_{a,\dots,T_{2}^{j-1}a}(\varphi)G_{a}(g)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\varepsilon(h)Q_{a}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)G_{a}(g)Q_{T_{2}a}\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)\cdots Q_{T_{2}^{j-1}a}\bigl(\varphi_{(j)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{QP comm rel}}}{{=}}{}

\displaystyle\varepsilon(h)G_{a}\bigl(g_{(1)}\bigr)Q_{a}\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\leftharpoonup g_{(2)}\bigr)Q_{T_{2}a}\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)\cdots Q_{T_{2}^{j-1}a}\bigl(\varphi_{(j)}\bigr)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle G_{a}\bigl(g_{(1)}\bigr)\varepsilon(h)Q_{a,\dots,T_{2}^{j-1}a}\bigl(\varphi\leftharpoonup g_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle G_{a}\bigl(g_{(1)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{1}}\bigl(\Phi\leftharpoonup\bigl(\varepsilon\otimes g_{(2)}\bigr)\bigr),

which, together with (
7.13
), is sufficient to conclude that (
7.17
) holds also if
\upsilon_{1}
is a type 2 arc.

Case
\bm{F_{a}}
with
\bm{\upsilon_{1}}
if
\bm{\upsilon_{1}}
is type 2.
With the same
\upsilon_{1}
as in the previous case, we can write

\displaystyle Q_{a,\dots,T_{2}^{j-1}a}(\varphi)F_{a}(\psi)

\displaystyle{}=Q_{a,\dots,T_{2}^{j-1}a}\bigl(\varphi\psi_{(1)}\bigr)Q_{T_{2}^{j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{n-1}a}\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle{}=Q_{T_{2}^{j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{n-1}a}\bigl(\psi_{(4)}\bigr)Q_{a,\dots,T_{2}^{j-1}a}\bigl(\psi_{(3)}\bigr)Q_{a,\dots,T_{2}^{j-1}a}\bigl(S\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\bigr)\varphi\psi_{(1)}\bigr)

\displaystyle{}=F_{a}\bigl(\psi_{(3)}\bigr)Q_{a,\dots,T_{2}^{j-1}a}\bigl(S\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\bigr)\varphi\psi_{(1)}\bigr).

Case
\bm{F_{a}}
with
\bm{\upsilon_{2}}
if
\bm{\upsilon_{1}}
is type 2.
With
\upsilon_{1}
as in the previous two cases the next arc is

\upsilon_{2}=\bigl(\bigl(T_{2}^{j}a\bigr)_{0}^{-},\bigl(T_{0}^{-1}T_{2}^{j}a\bigr)_{0}^{-},\dots,\bigl(T_{0}^{-k+1}T_{2}^{j}a\bigr)_{0}^{-}\bigr)
, where
0<k<\big|\mathcal{O}_{0}\bigl(T_{2}^{j}a\bigr)\big|
. Non-commutativity of
F_{a}
and
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{2}}

is due to the first arrow of
\upsilon_{2}
and to the arrow
\bigl(T_{2}^{j}a\bigr)_{2}^{+}
of
\gamma_{a}
,

\displaystyle P^{S}_{T_{2}^{j}a,\dots,T_{0}^{-k+1}T_{2}^{j}a}(h)F_{a}(\psi)=P_{T_{0}^{-k+1}T_{2}^{j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{j}a}(S(h))Q_{a,\dots,T_{2}^{j-1}a}\bigl(\psi_{(1)}\bigr)Q_{T_{2}^{j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{n-1}a}\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=Q_{a,\dots,T_{2}^{j-1}a}\bigl(\psi_{(1)}\bigr)P_{T_{0}^{-k+1}T_{2}^{j}a,\dots,T_{0}^{-1}T_{2}^{j}a}(S\bigl(h_{(2)}\bigr))P_{T_{2}^{j}a}\bigl(S\bigl(h_{(1)}\bigr)\bigr)Q_{T_{2}^{j}a}\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}\times Q_{T_{2}^{j+1}a,\dots,T_{2}^{n-1}a}\bigl(\psi_{(3)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad\ \ {}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{PQ lin}}}{{=}}Q_{a,\dots,T_{2}^{j-1}a}\bigl(\psi_{(1)}\bigr)P_{T_{0}^{-k+1}T_{2}^{j}a,\dots,T_{0}^{-1}T_{2}^{j}a}\bigl(S\bigl(h_{(2)}\bigr)\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}\times Q_{T_{2}^{j}a}\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\leftharpoonup S\bigl(S\bigl(h_{(1)}\bigr)_{(2)}\bigr)\bigr)P_{T_{2}^{j}a}\bigl(S\bigl(h_{(1)}\bigr)_{(1)}\bigr)Q_{T_{2}^{j+1}a,\dots,T_{2}^{n-1}a}\bigl(\psi_{(3)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=Q_{a,\dots,T_{2}^{j-1}a}\bigl(\psi_{(1)}\bigr)Q_{T_{2}^{j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{n-1}a}\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\leftharpoonup h_{(1)}\bigr)P_{T_{0}^{-k+1}T_{2}^{j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{j}a}\bigl(S\bigl(h_{(2)}\bigr)\bigr).

Combining this with the result of the previous case, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{1}\upsilon_{2}}(\Phi)F_{a}(\psi)=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{1}}\bigl(h_{(1)}\otimes\xi_{i}\varphi_{(1)}\xi_{j}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{2}}\bigl(S(x_{j})h_{(2)}x_{i}\otimes\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)F_{a}(\psi)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=Q_{a,\dots,T_{2}^{j-1}a}(\xi_{i}\varphi\xi_{j})P^{S}_{T_{2}^{j}a,\dots,T_{0}^{-k+1}T_{2}^{j}a}(S(x_{j})hx_{i})F_{a}(\psi)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=Q_{a,\dots,T_{2}^{j-1}a}(\xi_{i}\varphi\xi_{j})Q_{a,\dots,T_{2}^{j-1}a}\bigl(\psi_{(1)}\bigr)Q_{T_{2}^{j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{n-1}a}\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\leftharpoonup[S(x_{j})hx_{i}]_{(1)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}\times P_{T_{0}^{-k+1}T_{2}^{j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{j}a}\bigl(S\bigl([S(x_{j})hx_{i}]_{(2)}\bigr)\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=Q_{a,\dots,T_{2}^{j-1}a}\bigl(\xi_{i}\varphi\xi_{j}\psi_{(1)}\bigr)Q_{T_{2}^{j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{n-1}a}\bigl(\psi_{(3)}\bigr)P^{S}_{T_{2}^{j}a,\dots,T_{0}^{-k+1}T_{2}^{j}a}\bigl([S(x_{j})hx_{i}]\leftharpoonup\psi_{(2)}\bigr)

The Hopf algebraic identity

\xi_{i}\varphi\xi_{j}\psi_{(1)}\otimes[S(x_{j})hx_{i}]\leftharpoonup\psi_{(2)}=\psi_{(2)}\xi_{i}\varphi\xi_{j}\otimes S(x_{j})(h\leftharpoonup\psi_{(1)})x_{i}

shows that the last line above equals to

\displaystyle Q_{a,\dots,T_{2}^{j-1}a}\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\xi_{i}\varphi\xi_{j}\bigr)Q_{T_{2}^{j}a,\dots,T_{2}^{n-1}a}\bigl(\psi_{(3)}\bigr)P^{S}_{T_{2}^{j}a,\dots,T_{0}^{-k+1}T_{2}^{j}a}\bigl(S(x_{j})\bigl(h\leftharpoonup\psi_{(1)}\bigr)x_{i}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=F_{a}\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\bigr)Q_{a,\dots,T_{2}^{j-1}a}(\xi_{i}\varphi\xi_{j})P^{S}_{T_{2}^{j}a,\dots,T_{0}^{-k+1}T_{2}^{j}a}\bigl(S(x_{j})\bigl(h\leftharpoonup\psi_{(1)}\bigr)x_{i}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=F_{a}\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{1}}\bigl(\bigl(h\leftharpoonup\psi_{(1)}\bigr)_{(1)}\otimes\xi_{i}\varphi_{(1)}\xi_{j}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{2}}\bigl(S(x_{j})\bigl(h\leftharpoonup\psi_{(1)}\bigr)_{(2)}x_{i}\otimes\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=F_{a}\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{1}\upsilon_{2}}\bigl(h\leftharpoonup\psi_{(1)}\otimes\varphi\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=F_{a}\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\upsilon_{1}\upsilon_{2}}\bigl(\Phi\leftharpoonup\bigl(\psi_{(1)}\otimes 1\bigr)\bigr).

Taking into account (
7.16
), this proves (
7.18
) for

\rho
having first arc of type 2.

The above cases together prove formula (
7.11
) for all proper left ribbons with disjoint source and target.

Formula (
7.12
) requires to consider another 6 cases but there is a shorter and more instructive way to prove it.
We can apply duality to view the holonomy of the
left ribbon
\rho
as the holonomy of a right ribbon in another Kitaev model based on the Hopf algebra
H^{*}
. This duality has two ingredients: an algebra isomorphism

I\colon\mathcal{M}(\Sigma,H)\to\mathcal{M}(\Sigma^{*},H^{*})
and an isomorphism
J\colon\operatorname{Qv}(\mathcal{D}(P)^{\sim})\to\operatorname{Qv}(\mathcal{D}(P^{\backsim})^{\sim})
of quivers.
The
I
is defined by
I(P_{a}(h)):=Q^{*}_{T_{1}a}(h)
,
I(Q_{a}(\varphi)):=P^{*}_{a}(\varphi)
where the
P_{a}^{*}
and
Q_{a}^{*}
are the standard generators of
\mathcal{M}(\Sigma^{*},H^{*})

like the
P_{a}
and
Q_{a}
have been for
\mathcal{M}(\Sigma,H)
in Section
3
. Comparing equations (
3.7
) and (
3.18
),
it is easy to see that
I
is an algebra isomorphism.
It maps Gauss’ law operators to flux operators and vice versa,

\displaystyle I(G_{a}(h))=F^{*}_{T^{-1}_{2}a}(h),\qquad I(F_{a}(\varphi))=G^{*}_{T^{-1}_{2}a}(\varphi),\qquad I(\mathbf{D}_{a}(X))=\mathbf{D}^{*}_{T^{-1}_{2}a}(\Upsilon(X)),

where

\displaystyle\Upsilon\colon\ \mathcal{D}(H)

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{D}(H^{*}),

\displaystyle\varphi\otimes h

\displaystyle\mapsto h_{(2)}\otimes\varphi_{(2)}\cdot\bigl\bm{}{\langle}h_{(3)},\varphi_{(1)}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}\bigl\bm{}{\langle}S\bigl(h_{(1)}\bigr),\varphi_{(3)}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}

is an isomorphism of algebras and antiisomorphism of coalgebras.

In order to construct
J
, we first define a bijection
j
between the OCPMs associated to the arrow presentations
P
and its dual
P^{\backsim}
of (
4.2
) by

\displaystyle j\colon\ \Sigma(\mathrm{P})\to\Sigma(\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}{\mathrm{P}}),\qquad\mathcal{O}_{0}(a)\mapsto\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{2}(T_{1}a),\qquad\mathcal{O}_{1}(a)\mapsto\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{1}(a),\qquad\mathcal{O}_{2}(a)\mapsto\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}}{{\mathcal{O}}}_{0}(a),

which satisfies
