\displaystyle j\,\circ\,\partial_{0}=\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}}{{d}}_{1},\qquad j\,\circ\,d_{0}=\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}}{{\partial}}_{0},\qquad j\,\circ\,\partial_{1}=\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}}{{d}}_{0},\qquad j\,\circ\,d_{1}=\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}}{{\partial}}_{1}.

The definition of
J
is this

\displaystyle J\colon\ \operatorname{Qv}(\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{P})^{\sim})\to\operatorname{Qv}\bigl(\mathcal{D}\bigl(\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}{\mathrm{P}}\bigr)^{\sim}\bigr),\qquad\!\!\bm{\langle}v,f\bm{\rangle}\mapsto\bm{\langle}j(f),j(v)\bm{\rangle},\qquad\!\!a_{0}^{\pm}\mapsto(T_{1}a)_{2}^{\pm},\qquad\!\!a_{2}^{\pm}\mapsto a_{0}^{\pm}.

This can be shown to be a map of quivers by reading off the definition of
\nabla_{i}
from (
5.1
). For example,

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}}{{\nabla}}_{0}\,\circ\,J\bigl(a_{0}^{+}\bigr)

\displaystyle{}=\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}}{{\nabla}}_{0}(T_{1}a)_{2}^{+}=\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}}{{\partial}}_{0}T_{1}a,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}}{{d}}_{0}T_{1}a\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}=\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}}{{\partial}}_{1}a,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}}{{d}}_{1}a\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}

\displaystyle{}=\bm{\langle}j(d_{1}a),j(\partial_{0}a)\bm{\rangle}=J(\bm{\langle}\partial_{0}a,d_{1}a\bm{\rangle})=J\,\circ\,\nabla_{0}\bigl(a_{0}^{+}\bigr).

This
J
is one of the maps that realizes the intuitively clear idea that the complexes
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
and
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma^{*})^{*}
are the “same” except relabelling the arrows
and repainting the blue and red faces to red and blue, respectively.

Now
J
induces an isomorphism
J^{*}\colon\mathsf{Path}\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{P})^{\sim}\to\mathsf{Path}\mathcal{D}(\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}{\mathrm{P}})^{\sim}
of groupoids which sends a left ribbon
\rho\colon s(a)\to s(b)
to a right ribbon

J^{*}\rho\colon\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}}{{s}}\!\bigl(T_{2}^{-1}a\bigr)\to\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}}{{s}}\!\bigl(T_{2}^{-1}b\bigr)
.

(
The appearance of
T_{2}^{-1}
is due to the fact that the site labelling function

a\mapsto s(a)
satisfies
J\,\circ\,s=\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}}{{s}}\,\circ\,T_{2}^{-1}
. As such, it belongs to the object map of the functor
J^{*}
.
)

If
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ\,\backsim}
denotes the opholonomy of the dual model, then the duality formula says that for all curves
\rho
of the original model

I\bigl(\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)\bigr)=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ\,\backsim}_{J^{*}\rho}(\Upsilon^{*}(\Phi)),

(7.19)

where
\Upsilon^{*}=\bigl(\Upsilon^{\mathsf{T}}\bigr)^{-1}\colon\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}\to\mathcal{D}(H^{*})^{*}
is the mapping

\Upsilon^{*}(h\otimes\varphi)=\xi_{k}\varphi\xi_{l}\otimes S(x_{l})hx_{k},

which is an antiisomorphism of algebras and an isomorphism of coalgebras.

Applying the duality formula to (
7.11
), we obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ\,\backsim}_{J^{*}\rho}(\Upsilon^{*}(\Phi))\mathbf{D}^{*}_{T^{-1}_{2}a}(\Upsilon(X))

\displaystyle{}=\mathbf{D}^{*}_{T^{-1}_{2}a}\bigl(\Upsilon\bigl(X_{(1)}\bigr)\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ\,\backsim}_{J^{*}\rho}\bigl(\Upsilon^{*}\bigl(\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)\bigr)\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\Upsilon^{*}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr),\Upsilon\bigl(X_{(2)}\bigr)\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}

\displaystyle{}=\mathbf{D}^{*}_{T^{-1}_{2}a}\bigl(\Upsilon(X)_{(2)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ\,\backsim}_{J^{*}\rho}\bigl(\Upsilon^{*}(\Phi)_{(2)})\bm{\langle}\Upsilon^{*}(\Phi)_{(1)},\Upsilon(X)_{(1)}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}

\displaystyle{}=\mathbf{D}^{*}_{T^{-1}_{2}a}\bigl(\Upsilon(X)_{(2)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ\,\backsim}_{J^{*}\rho}\bigl(\Upsilon^{*}(\Phi)\leftharpoonup\Upsilon(X)_{(1)}\bigr).

Therefore,

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ\,\backsim}_{\gamma}(\Psi)\mathbf{D}^{*}_{a}(Y)=\mathbf{D}^{*}_{a}\bigl(Y_{(2)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ\,\backsim}_{\gamma}\bigl(\Psi\leftharpoonup Y_{(1)}\bigr)

for all proper right ribbons
\gamma\colon\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}}{{s}}\!(a)\to
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}}{{s}}\!(b)
, for
Y\in\mathcal{D}(H^{*})
and for
\Psi\in\mathcal{D}(H^{*})^{*}
. For the left ribbon

\gamma^{-1}
, this implies

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}^{*}_{a}(Y)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ\,\backsim}_{\gamma^{-1}}(\Psi)

\displaystyle{}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ\,\backsim}_{\gamma^{-1}}\bigl(\Psi_{(1)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ\,\backsim}_{\gamma}\bigl(\Psi_{(2)}\bigr)\mathbf{D}^{*}_{a}(Y)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ\,\backsim}_{\gamma^{-1}}\bigl(\Psi_{(3)}\bigr)

\displaystyle{}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ\,\backsim}_{\gamma^{-1}}\bigl(\Psi_{(1)}\bigr)\mathbf{D}^{*}_{a}\bigl(Y_{(2)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ\,\backsim}_{\gamma}\bigl(\Psi_{(2)}\leftharpoonup Y_{(1)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ\,\backsim}_{\gamma^{-1}}\bigl(\Psi_{(3)}\bigr)

\displaystyle{}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ\,\backsim}_{\gamma^{-1}}\bigl(Y_{(1)}\rightharpoonup\Psi\bigr)\mathbf{D}^{*}_{a}\bigl(Y_{(2)}\bigr).

Since this is true for all left ribbons
\gamma^{-1}
with target
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\backsim$}}}{{s}}\!(a)
not only on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma^{*})^{*}
and not only for
H^{*}
but on the dual
of the double of any OCPM
\Sigma
and for any involutive f.d. Hopf algebra
H
, we can apply it to the original
\Sigma
and
H
and we get the proof of
(
7.12
).
∎

For sake of completeness, we note here the formulas that hold instead of (
7.9
) and (
7.10
)
if
\rho\colon s(a)\to s(b)
is a proper right ribbon,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)\underset{\scriptscriptstyle a}{\blacktriangleleft}X=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi\leftharpoonup X),

(7.20)

\displaystyle X\underset{\scriptscriptstyle b}{\blacktriangleright}\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(X\rightharpoonup\Phi).

(7.21)

Corollary 7.7
.

Let
s(a)
and
s(b)
be disjoint sites and
\rho\colon s(a)\to s(b)
be a proper left ribbon.
Then the space
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\mathcal{D}(H)^{*})
of ribbon operators over
\rho
equipped with
the actions
\underset{\scriptscriptstyle b}{\triangleright}
and
\underset{\scriptscriptstyle a}{\triangleleft}
is a bimodule over
\mathcal{D}(H)
such that

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}\colon\ \mathcal{D}(H)^{*}\to\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\mathcal{D}(H)^{*})

is a homomorphism of
\mathcal{D}(H)
-
\mathcal{D}(H)
bimodules if we consider
\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
as the regular bimodule
\bm{\langle}\mathcal{D}(H)^{*},\rightharpoonup,\leftharpoonup\bm{\rangle}
.
This map is also a homomorphism of left
\mathcal{D}(H)
-module algebras and of right
\mathcal{D}(H)
-module algebras.

Similar holds for proper right ribbons
\rho\colon s(a)\to s(b)
but then the bimodule structure is given by
\underset{\scriptscriptstyle b}{\blacktriangleright}
and
\underset{\scriptscriptstyle a}{\blacktriangleleft}

and the algebra structure by
\mathcal{D}(H)^{*\,\mathrm{op}}
.

Proof.

That
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\mathcal{D}(H)^{*})
is a left and right
\mathcal{D}(H)
-module and that the map is a left and right
\mathcal{D}(H)
-module map follow from
Theorem
7.6
. Due to disjointness of the source and target sites, the left and right actions commute so

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\mathcal{D}(H)^{*})
is a bimodule. The last sentence follows from (
7.4
) which shows
that
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\mathcal{D}(H)^{*})\subset\mathcal{M}
is a left and right module subalgebra.
∎

Similar results can be obtained
[
5
]
in a context of non-semisimple Hopf algebras.

We close the section with two more results on closed ribbon operators.
An opcurve
\gamma\colon s_{0}\to s_{1}
is called closed if
s_{0}=s_{1}
.
If
\gamma=(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})
is closed, then every cyclic permutation

C^{k}\gamma=(d_{k+1},\dots,d_{n},d_{1},\dots,d_{k})
is also closed. It follows from the code word representation of ribbons that if the closed curve
\gamma
happens to be a ribbon then the
C^{k}\gamma
are also ribbons of the same type (left/right). Similarly, the cyclic permutations of proper closed ribbons are again proper as it is clear from
Definition
6.1
.

Proposition 7.8
.

Let
\delta\colon s(a)\to s(a)
and
\rho\colon s(a)\to s(a)
be closed left ribbons of the following form
(
for
\underset{\text{U}}{\leftrightarrow}
see Definition
7.1
)
:

\displaystyle\delta=\bigl(a_{0}^{-}\bigr)\delta^{\prime}\bigl(\bigl(T_{2}^{-1}a\bigr)_{2}^{+}\bigr),\qquad\text{where}\quad\delta^{\prime}\underset{\text{U}}{\leftrightarrow}\rho,

\displaystyle\rho=\bigl(a_{2}^{+}\bigr)\rho^{\prime}\bigl((T_{0}a)_{0}^{-}\bigr),\qquad\text{where}\quad\rho^{\prime}\underset{\text{U}}{\leftrightarrow}\lambda,

so that the only intersection of
\delta
and
\rho
is the site
s(a)
.
(
This is possible if the genus of
[\Sigma]
is at least
1.)
Then

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\delta}(\Psi)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(R_{2^{\prime}}\rightharpoonup\Phi\leftharpoonup R_{1})\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\delta}(R_{1^{\prime}}\rightharpoonup\Psi\leftharpoonup R_{2})

(7.22)

for all
\Phi,\Psi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
where
R=R_{1}\otimes R_{2}\equiv R_{1^{\prime}}\otimes R_{2^{\prime}}
is the
R
-matrix of
\mathcal{D}(H)
.

Proof.

For any curve
\gamma
we abbreviate
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma}
by
H_{\gamma}
.
The strategy of the proof is similar to that of Lemma
3.1
. We decompose the opholonomies into 3 terms,

\displaystyle H_{\delta}(\Psi)=H_{(a_{0}^{-})}\bigl(\Psi_{(1)}\bigr)\,H_{\delta^{\prime}}\bigl(\Psi_{(2)}\bigr)\,H_{((T_{2}^{-1}a)_{2}^{+})}\bigl(\Psi_{(3)}\bigr),

\displaystyle H_{\rho}(\Phi)=H_{(a_{2}^{+})}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)\,H_{\rho^{\prime}}\bigl(\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)\,H_{((T_{0}a)_{0}^{-})}\bigl(\Phi_{(3)}\bigr)

with the middle terms commuting with all terms of the other line. Then we exchange
H_{\delta}
and
H_{\rho}
in such a way that the order of terms
of the same line remains intact in every step. Using the identities (
6.5
) and (
6.6
), we can write

\displaystyle H_{\delta}(\Psi)H_{\rho}(\Phi)

\displaystyle{}=H_{(a_{0}^{-})}\bigl(\Psi_{(1)}\bigr)H_{(a_{2}^{+})}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)H_{\rho^{\prime}}\bigl(\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad{}\cdot H_{\delta^{\prime}}\bigl(\Psi_{(2)}\bigr)H_{((T_{2}^{-1}a)_{2}^{+})}\bigl(\Psi_{(3)}\bigr)H_{((T_{0}a)_{0}^{-})}\bigl(\Phi_{(3)}\bigr)

\displaystyle{}=H_{(a_{2}^{+})}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\leftharpoonup R_{1}\bigr)H_{(a_{0}^{-})}\bigl(\Psi_{(1)}\leftharpoonup R_{2}\bigr)H_{\rho^{\prime}}\bigl(\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)H_{\delta^{\prime}}\bigl(\Psi_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad{}\cdot H_{((T_{0}a)_{0}^{-})}\bigl(R_{2^{\prime}}\rightharpoonup\Phi_{(3)}\bigr)H_{((T_{2}^{-1}a)_{2}^{+})}\bigl(R_{1^{\prime}}\rightharpoonup\Psi_{(3)}\bigr)

\displaystyle{}=H_{(a_{2}^{+})}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\leftharpoonup R_{1}\bigr)H_{\rho^{\prime}}\bigl(\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)H_{((T_{0}a)_{0}^{-})}\bigl(R_{2^{\prime}}\rightharpoonup\Phi_{(3)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad{}\cdot H_{(a_{0}^{-})}\bigl(\Psi_{(1)}\leftharpoonup R_{2}\bigr)H_{\delta^{\prime}}\bigl(\Psi_{(2)}\bigr)H_{((T_{2}^{-1}a)_{2}^{+})}\bigl(R_{1^{\prime}}\rightharpoonup\Psi_{(3)}\bigr)

\displaystyle{}=H_{\rho}\bigl(R_{2^{\prime}}\rightharpoonup\Phi\leftharpoonup R_{1}\bigr)H_{\delta}\bigl(R_{1^{\prime}}\rightharpoonup\Psi\leftharpoonup R_{2}\bigr),

which completes the proof of (
7.22
).
∎

A closed ribbon version of (
7.9
) and (
7.10
) can be obtained as follows. First, we need a lemma.

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
\rho\colon s(a)\to s(a)
be a non-empty closed proper left ribbon which is not a face loop. Then there exist proper left ribbons
\rho_{1}\colon s(a)\to s(b)
and

\rho_{2}\colon s(b)\to s(a)
with disjoint
s(a)
and
s(b)
such that
\rho=\rho_{1}\rho_{2}
.

Proof.

Let
\rho=\upsilon_{1}\cdots\upsilon_{m}
be the arc decomposition as in the prof of Theorem
7.6
. Since
\rho
is not a face loop,
m\geq 2
. Let

s(a_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\upsilon_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}s(a_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\upsilon_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}s(a_{2})
, where
a_{0}=a
. Then
s(a_{1})
is neighbour to
s(a_{0})
and
s(a_{2})
is disjoint from
s(a_{0})
. Similarly, the sites of

s(a_{m-2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\upsilon_{m-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}s(a_{m-1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\upsilon_{m}}}{{\longrightarrow}}s(a_{m})
, where
a_{m}=a
, satisfy that
s(a_{m-1})
is neighbour to
s(a_{m})
and
s(a_{m-2})
is disjoint from

s(a_{m})
. This already excludes
m=2
and
m=3
. So we can take
\rho_{1}=\upsilon_{1}\upsilon_{2}
and
\rho_{2}=\upsilon_{3}\cdots\upsilon_{m}
.
∎

Proposition 7.10
.

Let
\rho\colon s(a)\to s(a)
be a non-empty closed proper left ribbon. Then

\mathbf{D}_{a}(X)\operatorname{Hol}_{\rho}(\Phi)=\operatorname{Hol}_{\rho}\bigl(X_{(1)}\rightharpoonup\Phi\leftharpoonup S_{\mathcal{D}}\bigl(X_{(3)}\bigr)\bigr)\mathbf{D}_{a}\bigl(X_{(2)}\bigr)

(7.23)

holds true for every
X\in\mathcal{D}(H)
and
\Phi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
.

Proof.

First, we assume that
\rho
is not a face loop.
Take a decomposition

\rho=s(a)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}s(b)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rho_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}s(a)

provided by Lemma
7.9
with disjoint sites
s(a)
and
s(b)
.
Using Theorem
7.6
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{a}(X)\operatorname{Hol}_{\rho}(\Phi)

\displaystyle{}=\mathbf{D}_{a}(X)\operatorname{Hol}_{\rho_{1}}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}_{\rho_{2}}\bigl(\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle{}=\operatorname{Hol}_{\rho_{1}}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\leftharpoonup S_{\mathcal{D}}\bigl(X_{(2)}\bigr)\bigr)\mathbf{D}_{a}\bigl(X_{(1)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}_{\rho}\bigl(\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle{}=\operatorname{Hol}_{\rho_{1}}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\leftharpoonup S_{\mathcal{D}}\bigl(X_{(3)}\bigr)\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}_{\rho}\bigl(X_{(1)}\rightharpoonup\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)\mathbf{D}_{a}\bigl(X_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle{}=\operatorname{Hol}_{\rho}\bigl(X_{(1)}\rightharpoonup\Phi\leftharpoonup S_{\mathcal{D}}\bigl(X_{(3)}\bigr)\bigr)\mathbf{D}_{a}\bigl(X_{(2)}\bigr)

the statement is proven.

If
\rho
is a face loop, then
\rho
is either
\gamma_{a}
or
\alpha^{-1}_{a}
. Assume
\rho=\gamma_{a}
. Then by Proposition
6.5

\operatorname{Hol}_{\rho}(h\otimes\varphi)=\varepsilon(h)F_{a}(\varphi)=\mathbf{D}_{a}(\varphi\otimes 1)\,\varepsilon(h),

so the left-hand side of (
7.23
) with
X=\psi\otimes g
and
\Phi=h\otimes\varphi
can be written as

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{a}(X)\operatorname{Hol}_{\rho}(\Phi)=D_{a}(X(\varphi\otimes 1)\varepsilon(h)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathbf{D}_a(X)\operatorname{Hol}_\rho(\Phi)}{}=\mathbf{D}_{a}\bigl(\psi\varphi_{(2)}\otimes g_{(2)}\bigr)\cdot\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\varphi_{(3)},g_{(1)}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\varphi_{(1)},S\bigl(g_{(3)}\bigr)\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}\varepsilon(h).

For the right-hand side, a much longer computation is necessary,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}_{\rho}\bigl(X_{(1)}\rightharpoonup\Phi\leftharpoonup S_{\mathcal{D}}\bigl(X_{(3)}\bigr)\bigr)\mathbf{D}_{a}\bigl(X_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\operatorname{Hol}_{\rho}\bigl(\big[\underset{h_{ji}}{\underbrace{S(x_{j})h_{(2)}x_{i}}}\otimes\varphi_{(2)}\big]_{(1)}\bigr)\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\big[S(x_{j})h_{(2)}x_{i}\otimes\varphi_{(2)}\big]_{(2)},X_{(1)}\bigl\bm{}{\rangle}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}\times\bigl\bm{}{\langle}h_{(1)}\otimes\xi_{i}\varphi_{(1)}\xi_{j},S_{\mathcal{D}}\bigl(X_{(3)}\bigr)\bigl\bm{}{\rangle}\mathbf{D}_{a}\bigl(X_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\operatorname{Hol}_{\rho}\bigl(h_{ji(1)}\otimes\xi_{k}\varphi_{(2)}\xi_{l}\bigr)\bigl\bm{}{\langle}S(x_{l})h_{ji(2)}x_{k}\otimes\varphi_{(3)},X_{(1)}\bigl\bm{}{\rangle}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}\times\bigl\bm{}{\langle}h_{(1)}\otimes\xi_{i}\varphi_{(1)}\xi_{j},S_{\mathcal{D}}\bigl(X_{(3)}\bigr)\bigl\bm{}{\rangle}\mathbf{D}_{a}\bigl(X_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\mathbf{D}_{a}\bigl(\xi_{k}\varphi_{(2)}\xi_{l}\otimes 1\bigr)\bigl\bm{}{\langle}S(x_{l})h_{ji}x_{k}\otimes\varphi_{(3)},\psi_{(3)}\otimes g_{(1)}\bigl\bm{}{\rangle}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}\times\bigl\bm{}{\langle}x_{n}S\bigl(h_{(1)}\bigr)S(x_{m})\otimes\xi_{m}S(\xi_{j})S\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)S(\xi_{i})\xi_{n},\psi_{(1)}\otimes g_{(3)}\bigl\bm{}{\rangle}\mathbf{D}_{a}\bigl(\psi_{(2)}\otimes g_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\psi_{(3)},S(x_{l})S(x_{j})h_{(2)}x_{i}x_{k}\bigl\bm{}{\rangle}\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\varphi_{(3)},g_{(1)}\bigl\bm{}{\rangle}\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\psi_{(1)},x_{n}S\bigl(h_{(1)}\bigr)S(x_{m})\bigl\bm{}{\rangle}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}\times\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\xi_{m}S(\xi_{j})S\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)S(\xi_{i})\xi_{n},g_{(3)}\bigl\bm{}{\rangle}\mathbf{D}_{a}\bigl(\xi_{k}\varphi_{(2)}\xi_{l}\psi_{(2)}\otimes g_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\psi_{(7)},h_{(2)}\bigl\bm{}{\rangle}\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\varphi_{(3)},g_{(1)}\bigl\bm{}{\rangle}\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\psi_{(2)},S\bigl(h_{(1)}\bigr)\bigl\bm{}{\rangle}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad{}\times\bigl\bm{}{\langle}S\bigl(\psi_{(3)}\bigr)\psi_{(6)}S\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\bigr)S\bigl(\psi_{(8)}\bigr)\psi_{(1)},g_{(3)}\bigl\bm{}{\rangle}\mathbf{D}_{a}\bigl(\psi_{(9)}\varphi_{(2)}S\bigl(\psi_{(5)}\bigr)\psi_{(4)}\otimes g_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\varepsilon(h)\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\varphi_{(3)},g_{(1)}\bigl\bm{}{\rangle}\bigl\bm{}{\langle}S\bigl(\varphi_{(1)}\bigr),g_{(3)}\bigl\bm{}{\rangle}\mathbf{D}_{a}\bigl(\psi\varphi_{(2)}\otimes g_{(2)}\bigr).

The case of
\rho=\alpha^{-1}_{a}
can be proven by a similar calculation.
∎

Note that (
7.23
) is identical to the multiplication rule in the double of the double,
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{D}(H))
.

8
Homotopy

In order to explain “topological invariance” of the Kitaev model, the first step is to look for an appropriate notion of homotopy of curves.
Eventually, we want to use homotopy on the complex
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
but the very notion should be given for an arbitrary
\Sigma
.

Definition 8.1
.

Let
\Sigma
be a connected OCPM with arrow presentation
\bm{\langle}\mathrm{Arr},T_{0},T_{2}\bm{\rangle}
.

(i)

Let
f
be a face of
\Sigma
. An
f
-loop
\lambda
is a closed opcurve
\bigl(a,T_{2}a,T_{2}^{2}a,\dots\bigr)
or its inverse where
a\in\mathrm{Arr}
is such that

\mathcal{O}_{2}(a)=f
.
The set of
f
-loops for all
f\in\Sigma^{2}
are called face loops.

(ii)

A lasso
\vartheta
is a conjugate of a face loop, i.e.,
\vartheta=\sigma\lambda\sigma^{-1}
, where
\lambda
is a face loop and
\sigma
is
arbitrary, provided
\partial_{1}\sigma=\partial_{0}\lambda
.
If
\lambda
is an
f
-loop then
\vartheta
is called an
f
-lasso.

(iii)

Let
\alpha
,
\beta
be parallel opcurves, i.e.,
\partial_{i}\alpha=\partial_{i}\beta
,
i=0,1
. A (combinatorial) homotopy from
\alpha
to
\beta
is a
sequence of lassos

(\vartheta_{N},\dots,\vartheta_{1})
such that

\beta\vartheta_{N}\dots\vartheta_{2}\vartheta_{1}=\alpha\qquad\text{as paths.}

Two opcurves are called homotopic, denoted by
\alpha\sim\beta
, if they are parallel and there is a homotopy from one of them to the other.

(iv)

A contraction of a closed curve
\alpha\colon v\to v
is a homotopy from
\alpha
to the trivial curve
(v)
. A closed curve is contractible if has a contraction.

(v)

If
(\vartheta_{N},\dots,\vartheta_{1})\colon\alpha\to\beta
is a homotopy with
\vartheta_{i}
being an
f_{i}
-lasso, then the support of this homotopy is the set of faces occurring in the list
(f_{i})
.

Remark 8.2
.

There are op-versions of the above definition of homotopy of opcurves:

•

In ophomotopy of opcurves
\alpha\to\beta
means
\alpha\vartheta_{1}\cdots\vartheta_{N}=\beta
.

•

In homotopy of curves
\alpha\to\beta
means
\vartheta_{N}\,\circ\,\cdots\,\circ\,\vartheta_{1}\,\circ\,\alpha=\beta
.

•

In ophomotopy of curves
\alpha\to\beta
means
\vartheta_{1}\,\circ\,\cdots\,\circ\,\vartheta_{N}\,\circ\,\beta=\alpha
.

Scholium 8.3
.

Let
\Sigma
be a connected OCPM. Homotopy of opcurves on
\Sigma
satisfies the following elementary properties:

(i)

If
\bm{\vartheta}=(\vartheta_{N},\dots,\vartheta_{1})
is a homotopy
\alpha\to\beta
then

\bm{\vartheta}^{-1}=\bigl(\vartheta_{1}^{-1},\dots,\vartheta_{N}^{-1}\bigr)
is a homotopy
\beta\to\alpha
.

(ii)

If
\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{\vartheta}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\beta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bm{\vartheta}^{\prime}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\gamma
are homotopies, then the composition

\bm{\vartheta}^{\prime}\,\circ\,\bm{\vartheta}:=\bigl(\vartheta^{\prime}_{M},\dots,\vartheta^{\prime}_{1},\vartheta_{N},\dots,\vartheta_{1}\bigr)

is a homotopy
\alpha\to\gamma
.

(iii)

If two opcurves are equal as paths, then they are homotopic.

(iv)

Homotopy is an equivalence relation on the set of opcurves and each path is the subset of a unique homotopy class.

(v)

A homotopy
\alpha\to\beta
is the same as a contraction of
\beta^{-1}\alpha
.

(vi)

If
\bm{\vartheta}\colon\alpha\to\beta
is a homotopy, then
\bigl(\beta\vartheta_{N}\beta^{-1},\dots\beta\vartheta_{1}\beta^{-1}\bigr)
is a
homotopy
\beta^{-1}\to\alpha^{-1}
.

(vii)

Let

u\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\longrightarrow}}v\overset{\beta}{\underset{\gamma}{\parbox{43.0pt}{\begin{picture}\put(3.0,6.0){\vector{1}{0}{37.0}}\put(3.0,2.0){\vector{1}{0}{37.0}}\end{picture}}}}w\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta}}{{\longrightarrow}}z

be opcurves and let
\bm{\vartheta}\colon\beta\to\gamma
be a homotopy. Then

\bigl(\dots,\delta^{-1}\vartheta_{i}\delta,\dots\bigr)
is a homotopy
\alpha\beta\delta\to\alpha\gamma\delta
.

(viii)

If
(\vartheta_{N},\dots,\vartheta_{1})\colon\alpha\to\beta
is a homotopy and
p\in S_{N}
is any permutation, then there exist closed curves
\pi_{i}
such that

\vartheta^{\prime}_{i}:=\pi_{i}\vartheta_{p(i)}\pi_{i}^{-1}
defines a homotopy
(\vartheta^{\prime}_{N},\dots,\vartheta^{\prime}_{1})\colon\alpha\to\beta
.

The set of homotopy classes of opcurves together with concatenation is a groupoid which is the opposite of the traditional fundamental groupoid

\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
. It is evident from Definition
8.1
, although its formalization could be tedious, that this groupoid is the same as the
fundamental groupoid
\pi_{1}([\Sigma])
of the geometric realization of the complex
\Sigma
.

The notion of contraction defined above does not reflect the idea that a contractible curve bounds a disk and the support of the contraction is the disk itself. As a matter of fact, any finite set of faces can be the support of a contraction. So one should study simple curves and their simple homotopies.
But before we would dig too deeply into the subject of lasso homotopy we should remind ourselves of our main goal which is the algebra of ribbon operators on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
.
It turns out that the use of lassos in holonomy operators is extremely inconvenient. The lassos
\sigma\lambda\sigma^{-1}
are not proper
ribbons and the long tails
\sigma
introduce serious non-commutativities which practically obstructs any computation.
We need some other kind of homotopy specialized to ribbon curves on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
by means of which we can deform (proper) ribbons in such a way that the
curve stays a (proper) ribbon in each step.

Let us introduce the following open “lassos” (Figure
6
):

\displaystyle\kappa_{a}:=\bigl((T_{1}a)_{2}^{+},\bigl(T_{0}^{m-1}a\bigr)_{0}^{-},\dots,(T_{0}a)_{0}^{-},a_{2}^{+}\bigr)\colon\ s(T_{1}a)\to s(T_{2}a),

(8.1)

\displaystyle\lambda_{a}:=\bigl((T_{1}a)_{0}^{-},(T_{2}a)_{2}^{+},\dots,\bigl(T_{2}^{n-1}a\bigr)_{2}^{+},a_{0}^{-}\bigr)\colon\ s(T_{1}a)\to s\bigl(T_{0}^{-1}a\bigr),

(8.2)

where
m=|\mathcal{O}_{0}(a)|
and
n=|\mathcal{O}_{2}(a)|
. Note that
\kappa_{a},\lambda_{a}\in\mathsf{Ribb}^{\circ}_{L}
.

Figure 6:
The basic curves of ribbon homotopy.

Unlike the lassos the
\kappa
-s and
\lambda
-s cannot be nicely
composed. So the strategy in ribbon curve homotopy is not composing the elementary paths, it is more like the procedure of grafting trees in which

\kappa_{a}
and
\lambda_{a}
play the role of the grafts.

Remark 8.4
.

Speaking about subcurves and subpaths, in general, requires some care. For opcurves
\sigma
and
\rho
, the relation
\sigma\subset\rho
means the
existence of opcurves
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
such that
\rho=\rho_{1}\sigma\rho_{2}
as opcurves, hence no reduction is allowed. For paths of opcurves,

\sigma\subset\rho
means the existence of paths
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
such that
\rho=\rho_{1}\sigma\rho_{2}
as paths.
In case of both
\sigma
and
\rho
are ribbons (of the same type), the relation
\sigma\subset\rho
for the ribbon opcurves is stronger than the
same relation for their paths. However, if we think more categorically and utilize the fact that
\mathsf{Ribb}^{\circ}_{L/R}
is a category for concatenation, then
we can use the notion of subribbon as a third possibility. For left ribbons, e.g.,
\sigma\subset\rho
is a subribbon if there exist left ribbons

\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
such that
\rho=\rho_{1}\sigma\rho_{2}
either as opcurves or as paths because concatenation of left ribbons is always reduced
so these two alternatives are equivalent.
This third version of “subcurve” is which we need in the next definition.

Definition 8.5
.

(a)

For left ribbon opcurves
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\colon s_{0}\to s_{1}
, we say that the pair
(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
is an elementary contraction and that
(\rho^{\prime},\rho)

is an elementary relaxation in the following 4 cases:

•

Vertex contraction: For some
a\in\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)
the
\rho
contains a subribbon
\alpha_{a}^{-1}
(see (
6.7
)) and
\rho^{\prime}
is
obtained from
\rho
by discarding this
\alpha_{a}^{-1}
.

•

Face contraction: For some
a\in\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)
, the
\rho
contains a subribbon
\gamma_{a}
(see (
6.9
)) and
\rho^{\prime}
is
obtained from
\rho
by discarding this
\gamma_{a}
.

•

\kappa
-contraction: For some
a\in\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)
, the
\rho
contains a subribbon
\kappa_{a}\subset\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
is obtained by replacing this
subribbon by
\bigl((T_{1}a)_{0}^{-}\bigr)
.

•

\lambda
-contraction: For some
a\in\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)
, the
\rho
contains a subribbon
\lambda_{a}\subset\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
is obtained by replacing this
subribbon by
\bigl((T_{1}a)_{2}^{+}\bigr)
.

For
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Ribb}^{\circ}_{L}
, we write
\rho\approx\rho^{\prime}
and say that they are ribbon-homotopic if there is a sequence of left ribbons
(\rho_{0},\dots,\rho_{k})
,
the homotopy, such that
\rho_{0}=\rho
,
\rho_{k}=\rho^{\prime}
and each move
(\rho_{i},\rho_{i+1})
is an elementary contraction or an elementary relaxation.

(b)

For closed ribbons, we also define free ribbon-homotopy as a ribbon-homotopy in which we allow also circular moves:
(\rho_{i},\rho_{i+1})

is a circular move if
\rho_{i+1}
differs from
\rho_{i}
only in the choice of the base point. Free ribbon-homotopy is denoted by

\overset{\star}{\approx}
.

(c)

For right ribbons, the definitions (a) and (b) can be used without change except that in the definition of moves the curves
\alpha_{a}^{-1}
,
\gamma_{a}
,
\kappa_{a}
,
\lambda_{a}
have to be replaced by
\alpha_{a}
,
\gamma_{a}^{-1}
,
\kappa_{a}^{-1}
,
\lambda_{a}^{-1}
, respectively.

(d)

The support of a (free) ribbon-homotopy is the set
F\subseteq\Sigma
of faces of
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
arising as the union of the supports of the elementary
contractions/relaxations. In case of vertex or face contraction/relaxation, the support is just the vertex or face in question.
In case of
\kappa
-contraction/relaxation, the support has 2 elements, a vertex and an edge of
\Sigma
, the ones being winded around by
\kappa_{a}
.
For
\lambda
-contraction/relaxation, the support is a similarly defined pair of a face and of an edge.

Notice that we have not defined homotopy between a left and a right ribbon.

In order to place ribbon-homotopy into the wider context of homotopy, we need to see abundance of ribbons in some sense.

Proposition 8.6
.

Let
\Sigma
be a connected OCPM and let
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*0}
be two sites.

({\rm i})

There exists a left ribbon
\rho\colon s\to s^{\prime}
.

({\rm ii})

If
\gamma\colon s\to s^{\prime}
is an opcurve, then there exists a left ribbon
\rho\colon s\to s^{\prime}
such that
\gamma\sim\rho
, i.e.,
\gamma
and
\rho
are
homotopic in the sense of Definition
8.1
.

({\rm iii})

If
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\colon s\to s^{\prime}
are left ribbons such that
\rho_{1}\approx\rho_{2}
, then
\rho_{1}\sim\rho_{2}
.

Proof.

(ii) Let
\gamma
be the composite
s=s(a_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}s(a_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c_{n}}}{{\longrightarrow}}s(a_{n})=s^{\prime}
,
v_{k}=\mathcal{O}_{0}(a_{k})
,
f_{k}=\mathcal{O}_{2}(a_{k})
and let
w=W_{n}\cdots W_{1}

be the code word of
\gamma
. The proof follows the principle of replacing bad letters with good words.
Construct the new word

\displaystyle w^{\prime}:=w^{\prime}_{n}\cdots w^{\prime}_{1},\qquad\text{where}\quad w^{\prime}_{k}:=\begin{cases}W_{k}&\text{if}\ W_{k}\in\bigl\{T_{0}^{-1},T_{2}\bigr\},\\
T_{0}^{-|v_{k}|+1}&\text{if}\ W_{k}=T_{0},\\
T_{2}^{|f_{k}|-1}&\text{if}\ W_{k}=T_{2}^{-1},\end{cases}

and define the ribbons
\rho_{k}:=\bm{\langle}a_{0},w^{\prime}_{k}\cdots w^{\prime}_{1}\bm{\rangle}
. Then
\vartheta_{k}:=\rho_{k-1}(c_{k})\rho_{k}^{-1}
is a lasso such that

\vartheta_{1}\vartheta_{2}\cdots\vartheta_{n}=\gamma\rho_{n}^{-1}
. Therefore,
\rho=\rho_{n}
is the desired ribbon and

\bigl(\gamma^{-1}\vartheta_{1}\gamma,\dots,\gamma^{-1}\vartheta_{n}\gamma\bigr)\colon\gamma\to\rho
is the desired homotopy.

(i) Since
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
is connected, there exists an opcurve
\gamma=s\to s^{\prime}
. Thus, (i) follows from (ii).

(iii) It suffices to show that the elementary relaxations are homotopies. For the cases of vertex and face relaxations, this is obvious. For
\kappa_{a}
,
we can easily check the equality of paths
\bigl((T_{1}a)_{0}^{-}\bigr)=\kappa_{a}\vartheta_{v}\vartheta_{e}
with lassos
\vartheta_{v}=(a_{2}^{-})\alpha_{a}\bigl(a_{2}^{+}\bigr)
and

{\vartheta_{e}=(a_{2}^{-},a_{0}^{-})\beta_{a}^{-1}\bigl(a_{0}^{+},a_{2}^{+}\bigr)}
. Thus,
{(\vartheta_{v},\vartheta_{e})\colon\kappa_{a}\to\bigl((T_{1}a)_{0}^{-}\bigr)}
is a homotopy.
Similar formulas can be found for
\lambda_{a}
.
∎

Assume that we are given a closed ribbon which is contractible, e.g., because we know that the ribbon bounds a disk. How can we design a ribbon-homotopy
which shrinks the ribbon to zero? This is the kind of problem in which “tree curves” play a role.

Definition 8.7
.

Tree curves or, more descriptively, tree dressing curves are closed ribbon opcurves occurring in two types:

A tree curve
\tau_{F}
can be constructed from a tree
F\subset\Sigma^{0}\cup\Sigma^{1}
, as a subgraph of
\Sigma
, as follows. Considering

F
as a set of faces of
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
we take
\tau_{F}
to be any of the negatively oriented reduced boundary opcurves of this fattened tree
F
.

A dual tree curve
\tau_{F}
can be constructed from a dual tree
F\subset\Sigma^{2}\cup\Sigma^{1}
, as a subgraph of
\Sigma^{*}
, as follows. Consider

F
as a set of faces of
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
and take
\tau_{F}
to be any of the positively oriented reduced boundary opcurves of this fattened tree
F
.

Predecessors of the tree curves are the “dual blocks” and “direct blocks” of Bombin and Martin-Delgado
[
1
]
.
We need also rooted versions of them which are open ribbons:

Definition 8.8
.

A rooted tree curve is obtained from a tree curve
\tau_{F}
by discarding the maximal arc running around a leaf
vertex
v\in F
. This curve is denoted by
\tau_{F^{\prime}}
, where
F^{\prime}=F\setminus\{v\}
is the underlying rooted tree. The root of
\tau_{F^{\prime}}
is the arrow
which would complete the discarded arc to a full loop around
v
. If
r
is the root,
(r)
is called the root curve.

A rooted dual tree curve is obtained from a dual tree curve
\tau_{F}
by discarding the maximal arc running around a leaf
face
f\in F
. This curve is denoted by
\tau_{F^{\prime}}
, where
F^{\prime}=F\setminus\{f\}
is the underlying rooted dual tree. The root of
\tau_{F^{\prime}}
is
the arrow which would complete the discarded arc to a full loop around
f
. If
r
is the root,
(r)
is called the root curve.

Note the deviation from the usual notion of rooted tree. Here the root vertex is not an arbitrary vertex but a leaf. Furthermore, the root vertex
does not belong to the rooted tree, so in fact there is only a root edge which is characterized by having only one boundary vertex (a “half edge”).

The inverses
\tau^{-1}_{F}

\bigl(\tau^{-1}_{F^{\prime}}\bigr)
of the above (rooted) tree curves are needed in ribbon-homotopy of right ribbons,
so they should also be called (rooted) tree curves.

Lemma 8.9
.

The following facts are either obvious or easy to prove:

(i)

For every
(
rooted
)

(
dual
)
tree
F
, the
\tau_{F}
is a proper left ribbon and
\tau_{F}^{-1}
is a proper right ribbon.

(ii)

If
F
is a singleton tree in
\Sigma

(
in
\Sigma^{*})
, then the
(
dual
)
tree curve
\tau_{F}
is
\alpha_{a}^{-1}

(\gamma_{a})
for some
a\in\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)
.

(iii)

If the rooted tree
F^{\prime}
in
\Sigma

(
in
\Sigma^{*})
consists of
2
elements, then the rooted
(
dual
)
tree curve
\tau_{F^{\prime}}
is a
\kappa_{a}

(\lambda_{a})
for some
a\in\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)
.

(iv)

Every
(
dual
)
tree curve can be obtained from an
\alpha_{a}^{-1}

(\gamma_{a})
by successive grafting of
\kappa_{a}
-s
(\lambda_{a}
-s
)

by their roots to some arrows of color
T_{0}^{-1}

(T_{2})
. The same can be said about the rooted
(
dual
)
tree curves if we start with a
\kappa

(\lambda)
.

(v)

Every
\kappa_{a}
,
\lambda_{a}
is ribbon-homotopic to its root curve.

(vi)

Every tree curve and dual tree curve is ribbon-homotopic to some trivial ribbon.

(vii)

Every rooted tree curve and rooted dual tree curve is ribbon-homotopic to its root curve.

Under (iv) the “grafting” means a
\kappa
- or
\lambda
-relaxation under the condition that the result remains a proper opcurve. We do not have to use
unproper relaxations to get tree curves although a general ribbon-homotopy would allow them.

Theorem 8.10
.

For a connected OCPM
\Sigma
, let
\rho\colon s\to s
be a closed proper left ribbon.
We divide the faces of
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
into connected components by saying that two faces
p
and
q
of
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
are connected if there is a dual
curve
\delta\colon p\to q
on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
such that neither the arrows of
\delta
nor their opposites are members of
\rho
. Assume that

•

\rho
cuts the set of faces into
2
connected components,
L
consisting of the faces lying on the left and
R
consisting of the
faces lying on the right of
\rho
;

•

as subcomplexes of
\Sigma
either
L
or
R
has Euler characteristic
1
.

Then there is a ribbon-homotopy from
\rho
to the trivial ribbon
(s)
such that the support of the homotopy is
L
, resp.
R
, and in each step of the
homotopy the curve is proper.

Figure 7:
A plan for contracting a left ribbon. (See the proof of Theorem
8.10
.)

Proof.

Looking at the two connected components
L
and
R
as sets of cells of
\Sigma
, we have
L\cap R=\varnothing
and
L\cup R=\Sigma
. But there is a substantial difference
between these subcomplexes.
L
is upper semiclosed, i.e., together with any cell
c
it contains also
\operatorname{Cb}(c)
, while
R
is lower semiclosed
2
2

2

A subset of a connected CPM
\Sigma
which is both lower and upper semiclosed is either empty or the whole
\Sigma
.
,
i.e., together with any cell
c
it contains also
\operatorname{Bd}(c)
. This is visible on Figure
7
by the appearance of alternating
red and green dots on the left-hand side of
\rho
and of the blue and green dots on the right-hand side. These sequences of faces and edges on the left
and vertices and edges on the right are the boundary dual curve of
L
and the boundary curve of
R
, respectively. They coincide with the two
borderlines
E^{*}(\rho)=\bm{\langle}c,b\bm{\rangle}
of
\rho
, see in and below Lemma
5.4
. Since
\rho
is a ribbon, these borderlines never cross by
Lemma
5.8
.

Let
L^{d}=\Sigma^{d}\cap L
for
d=0,1,2
and assume that it is
L
which has Euler characteristic
\chi(L)=1
. Choose a maximal dual tree

F\subset L^{2}\cup L^{1}
in the connected complex
L
. Let
\tau_{F}
be its dual tree curve the base point of which is that of
\rho
.
Since
F
contains all faces of
L
but none of its vertices and
F
has
\chi(F)=1
, the complement
G:=L\setminus F
has Euler characteristic

\displaystyle\chi(G)=\bigl|G^{0}\bigr|-\bigl|G^{1}\bigr|=\bigl|L^{0}\bigr|-\bigl(\bigl|L^{1}\bigr|-\bigl(\bigl|L^{2}\bigr|-1\bigr)\!\bigr)=\bigl|L^{0}\bigr|-\bigl|L^{1}\bigr|+\bigl|L^{2}\bigr|-1=\!\chi(L)-1=0.

As a subgraph of the planar
L
the
G
cannot contain circles because they would separate some faces from the maximal dual tree
F
. Therefore,
G
is a disjoint union of trees
G=G_{1}\cup\dots\cup G_{k}
each of which has to be rooted in order to satisfy
\chi(G_{i})=0
.
The root edge of
G_{i}
, in the language of
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
, is a type 1 face
e_{i}
having one arrow
r_{i}\in\rho
of color
T_{0}^{-1}
on its boundary.
This is the root of
G_{i}
. Starting with the root
r_{i}
, we can perform a sequence of
\kappa
-relaxations along the tree
G_{i}
. Doing this for all
G_{i}
,
we arrive to that
\rho
is a ribbon-homotopic to
\tau_{F}
. Since
\tau_{F}
can be contracted by
\lambda
-contractions to the trivial curve, we are done.
During each steps of the homotopy we had only proper ribbons and used only grafts
\kappa_{a}
,
\lambda_{b}
which belonged entirely to
L
. Furthermore,
the supports of the used grafts form a partition of
L
into vertex-edge pairs and face-edge pairs, so the support of the homotopy is precisely
L
.
(In this case, we call
L
the contraction disk of
\rho
.)

If it is
R
which has
\chi(R)=1
, then the proof goes by constructing a maximal tree
F\subset R^{0}\cup R^{1}
and the forest of rooted dual trees

G=R\setminus F
. Then the homotopy first
\lambda
-relaxes
\rho
inside
R
along the forest and then
\kappa
-contracts the resulting tree
curve
\tau_{F}
.
∎

Figure 8:
The plan of a proper ribbon-homotopy from the blue to the red left ribbon. (See the proof of Theorem
8.13
.)

The special ribbon-homotopies we found in the above theorem turn out to be the only ribbon-homotopies applicable in the Kitaev model. So we give
them a name.

Definition 8.11
.

A (free) ribbon-homotopy
(\rho_{0},\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{n})
is called a proper (free) ribbon-homotopy if each
\rho_{i}
is proper (Definition
6.1
).
A proper ribbon which has a proper ribbon-homotopy to a trivial curve is called properly contractible.

Example 8.12
.

The figure 8 opcurve
8_{a}:=\alpha_{a}^{-1}\gamma_{a}
is a left ribbon which is not proper. It has a ribbon-homotopy to
(s(a))
but no proper ribbon-homotopy
at all. The same can be said about the double loop
\gamma_{a}\gamma_{a}
.

Theorem 8.13
.

Let
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\colon s(a)\to s(b)
be parallel non-trivial proper left ribbons such that the closed opcurve
\rho_{1}\rho_{2}^{-1}
is the positively oriented boundary
curve of a disk
B\subset\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*2}
. Then there is a proper ribbon-homotopy
\rho_{1}\to\rho_{2}
of support
B
.

Proof.

By assumption,
B
lies on the left of
\rho_{1}
and the right of
\rho_{2}
.
The
B
is now neither upper nor lower semiclosed and, strangely enough, has Euler characteristic 0 in
\Sigma
. This is an artifact of the different
behaviour at the two borderline segments
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
:

B
looks like upper semiclosed along the
\rho_{1}
and lower semiclosed along
\rho_{2}
.
So we can start growing
rooted trees (with roots on
\rho_{1}
necessarily) until we get a maximal forest
F\subset B
of rooted trees (see Figure
8
).
The complement
F^{*}=B\setminus F
is then
a maximal forest of rooted dual trees (with roots on
\rho_{2}
necessarily). The homotopy then starts with
\kappa
-relaxations of
\rho_{1}
along
the trees of
F
. The result is a proper left ribbon
\gamma\colon s(a)\to s(b)
which consists of the (broken) borderline between
F
and
F^{*}
completed by
those arrows of
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
which are not roots of some tree. Finally, a sequence of
\lambda
-contractions applied to
\gamma
along
F^{*}

results in the ribbon
\rho_{2}
.
∎

The intermediate curve
\gamma
in the proof of the above theorem has the remarkable property of passing through every site of the disk
B

exactly once. So it is a Hamiltonian opcurve on the graph
\downarrow\!B\setminus B
obtained by taking the lower semiclosed closure of
B
in the
complex
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
and then discarding all its faces.
The existence of such Hamiltonian curve is crucial in the following free ribbon-homotopy.

Theorem 8.14
.

Let
A
be a finite connected set of faces of
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
with Euler characteristic
0
in
\Sigma
such that the positively oriented boundary
of
A
in
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
consists of two components, a closed proper left ribbon
\rho_{1}
and a closed proper right ribbon
\rho_{2}^{-1}
.
Then there is a proper free ribbon-homotopy
\rho_{1}\to\rho_{2}
supported on the annulus
A
.

Proof.

The proof is very similar to the proof of Theorem
8.13
therefore we content ourselves with referring to Figure
9
.
∎

Figure 9:
For a free proper ribbon-homotopy from the blue ribbon to the red ribbon first choose a maximal forest of rooted trees on the annulus.
These are the bluish faces.
Then perform
\kappa
-relaxations on the blue ribbon along the bluish faces. What we get is a Hamilton cycle; a closed proper ribbon (not shown) visiting every site of the annulus exactly once. Then we make a circular move along the Hamilton cycle in order to transplant the base point from the
blue ribbon to the red ribbon. Finally, we perform
\lambda
-contractions along the reddish faces.

The annulus of Figure
9
is obtained by taking the relative complement of a disk within a larger disk. But this need not be so. The proof of Theorem
8.14
nowhere uses that the “hole” is a disk. So it applies to any annulus strip winding around a torus or on some handle of a
higher genus surface.

9
Ground states

So far the Kitaev model was treated on a pure algebraic level. Now we turn it into a model in quantum field theory. So we assume that the ground field

K=\mathbb{C}
, the field of complex numbers, and the finite-dimensional Hopf algebra
H
is a C
∗
-Hopf algebra. This means, on the one hand, that
H
is endowed
with an antilinear involution, called the star operation,
3
3

3

Having already many stars in this paper we shall denote the star operation, as well as the adjoints of bounded linear operators on a Hilbert space, by
(\cdot)^{\dagger}
.
satisfying

(gh)^{\dagger}=h^{\dagger}g^{\dagger},\quad{h_{(1)}}^{\dagger}\otimes{h_{(2)}}^{\dagger}=\bigl(h^{\dagger}\bigr)_{(1)}\otimes\bigl(h^{\dagger}\bigr)_{(2)},\qquad g,h\in H.

Consequently,

1^{\dagger}=1,\qquad\varepsilon\bigl(h^{\dagger}\bigr)=\overline{\varepsilon(h)},\qquad S(h)^{\dagger}=S^{-1}\bigl(h^{\dagger}\bigr),\qquad i^{\dagger}=i.

On the other hand, one requires that
H
has a faithful star-representation on a Hilbert space, i.e., such that
h^{\dagger}
is represented by the adjoint of
the operator representing
h
. From now on
H
denotes a finite-dimensional C
∗
-Hopf algebra. Finite-dimensional C
∗
-Hopf algebras are
semisimple and have
S^{2}=\operatorname{id}
. So every Hopf algebraic relations used before remain valid. In particular,
H^{*}
has a Haar integral
\iota
and the formula

(g,h):=\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\iota,g^{\dagger}h\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}

is a scalar product making
H
a Hilbert space and the left regular representation a faithful star representation,
(k,hg)=\bigl(h^{\dagger}k,g\bigr)
.

The dual Hopf algebra
H^{*}
is then also a C
∗
-Hopf algebra. The star operation of
H^{*}
is given by

\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\varphi^{\dagger},h\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}:=\overline{\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\varphi,S(h)^{\dagger}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}},\qquad\varphi\in H^{*},\quad h\in H.

It follows that the double
\mathcal{D}(H)
and its dual
\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
are both C
∗
-Hopf algebras with respective star operations

\displaystyle(\psi\otimes g)^{\dagger}=\psi^{\dagger}_{(2)}\otimes g^{\dagger}_{(2)}\cdot\overline{\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\psi_{(3)},S\bigl(g_{(1)}\bigr)\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}}\,\overline{\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\psi_{(1)},g_{(3)}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}},\qquad(h\otimes\varphi)^{\dagger}=h^{\dagger}\otimes\varphi^{\dagger}.

Assume
\Sigma
is a finite connected OCPM.
Since
\mathcal{M}(\Sigma)
is a full matrix algebra, it has, up to isomorphisms, only one irreducible representation. The standard choice for the Hilbert space of this representation is
\mathscr{H}(\Sigma):=H^{\otimes\Sigma^{1}}
which is the tensor product of copies
H_{e}
of the Hilbert space
H
for each edge

e\in\Sigma^{1}
.
The action of
\mathcal{M}
on
\mathscr{H}
is defined by means of an orientation of the edges, i.e., by a section
e\mapsto a_{e}
of the function

\mathcal{O}_{1}\colon\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)\to\Sigma^{1}
. Namely,
P_{a}(h)
and
Q_{a}(\varphi)
act as the identity on each tensor factor
H_{e}
,
e\neq\mathcal{O}_{1}(a)
, and
for
e=\mathcal{O}_{1}(a)
the
P_{a}(h)
is left multiplication by
h
and
Q_{a}(\varphi)
is
\varphi\rightharpoonup\mbox{\,\rm\_}\,
on
H_{e}
if
a=a_{e}
and

P_{a}(h)
is right multiplication by
S(h)
and
Q_{a}(\varphi)=\mbox{\,\rm\_}\,\leftharpoonup S(\varphi)
on
H_{e}
if
a=T_{1}a_{e}
; cf. (
3.10
) and (
3.11
).
In this way the abstract operators
P_{a}(h)
,
Q_{a}\bigl(\varphi\bigr)
become concrete operators on the Hilbert space
\mathscr{H}
such that
P_{a}(h)^{\dagger}=P_{a}\bigl(h^{\dagger}\bigr)

and
Q_{a}(\varphi)^{\dagger}=Q_{a}\bigl(\varphi^{\dagger}\bigr)
, where
(\cdot)^{\dagger}
on the right-hand side is the abstract star operation while on the left-hand side it is the adjoint of an operator
on
\mathscr{H}
.

The adjoints of one-step holonomies (
6.3
) satisfy
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d)}(\Psi)^{\dagger}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(d)}\bigl(\Psi^{\dagger}\bigr)
. Therefore, if
\gamma
is an arbitrary opcurve, then

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma}(\Psi)^{\dagger}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma^{-1}}\bigl(S_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}(\Psi)^{\dagger}\bigr),\qquad\Phi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}.

(9.1)

But if the curve is a proper ribbon, then we also have

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Psi)^{\dagger}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}\bigl(\Psi^{\dagger}\bigr),\qquad\Psi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*},\quad\text{if}\ \rho\ \text{is proper}.

(9.2)

In particular, the Gauss’ law and flux operators satisfy
G_{a}(h)^{\dagger}=G_{a}\bigl(h^{\dagger}\bigr)
and
F_{a}(\varphi)^{\dagger}=F_{a}\bigl(\varphi^{\dagger}\bigr)
. So
A_{v}
and
B_{f}

are self adjoint idempotents, i.e., projectors, and the Hamiltonian (
3.32
) is self adjoint, too.

The ground states of the Hamiltonian are represented by vectors
\Omega
in
\mathscr{H}
such that

A_{v}\Omega=\Omega\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_{f}\Omega=\Omega\qquad\forall v\in\Sigma^{0},\quad f\in\Sigma^{2}.

Following
[
12
]
, we denote by
\mathscr{P}
the subspace in
\mathscr{H}
of such vacuum vectors
\Omega
and call it the protected space.
Explicit wave functions of vacuum vectors have been calculated in
[
3
]
using a tensor network formalism.
If
H
is a group algebra of a finite group
G
, then a basis of
\mathscr{P}
consists of gauge equivalence classes of
G
-valued flat gauge fields on
\Sigma

[
5
]
. So the dimension of
\mathscr{P}
depends only on the genus of
\Sigma
through the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(\Sigma)
.
A far reaching generalization of the “protected object”
\mathscr{P}
appeared recently in
[
10
]
in which the Kitaev quantum double model is studied over Hopf monoids in monoidal categories.

In terms of expectation values
\omega(M)=(\Omega,\,M\Omega)
, the ground states can be abstractly characterized by the equations

\omega(MA_{v})=\omega(M)=\omega(MB_{f}),\qquad M\in\mathcal{M},\quad v\in\Sigma^{0},\quad f\in\Sigma^{2}

for the normalized (
\omega(\mathbf{1})=1
) positive linear functional
\omega\colon\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{C}
. Knowing
\omega
, which we do not, is complete information about
the representation on
\mathscr{H}
by the Gelfand–Neumark–Segal construction. So the representation of
\mathcal{M}
on
\mathscr{H}
may be called the vacuum representation.

The above properties of the vacua
\Omega\in\mathscr{P}
imply

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\alpha_{a}}(\Phi)\Omega

\displaystyle=\varphi(1)G_{a}(h)G_{a}(i)\Omega=\varphi(1)\varepsilon(h)\Omega=\Omega\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}(\Phi),

(9.3)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma_{a}}(\Phi)\Omega

\displaystyle=\varepsilon(h)F_{a}(\varphi)F_{a}(\iota)\Omega=\varepsilon(h)\varphi(1)\Omega=\Omega\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}(\Phi),

(9.4)

hence also the formula expressing gauge invariance of the vacua

\mathbf{D}_{a}(X)\Omega=\Omega\cdot\varepsilon_{D}(X),\qquad a\in\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma),\quad X\in\mathcal{D}(H),\quad\Omega\in\mathscr{P}.

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
a\in\mathrm{Arr}(\Sigma)
,
v=\mathcal{O}_{0}(a)
,
f=\mathcal{O}_{2}(a)
and let
\Omega\in\mathscr{H}
be any vector. Then for all
\Phi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\alpha^{\pm 1}_{a}}(\Phi)\Omega=\Omega\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}(\Phi)

\displaystyle\qquad\text{whenever}\quad A_{v}\Omega=\Omega,

(9.5)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma^{\pm 1}_{a}}(\Phi)\Omega=\Omega\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}(\Phi)

\displaystyle\qquad\text{whenever}\quad B_{f}\Omega=\Omega,

(9.6)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\kappa_{a}}(\Phi)\Omega=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{((T_{1}a)_{0}^{-})}\Omega

\displaystyle\qquad\text{whenever}\quad A_{v}\Omega=\Omega,

(9.7)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\lambda_{a}}(\Phi)\Omega=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{((T_{1}a)_{2}^{+})}\Omega

\displaystyle\qquad\text{whenever}\quad B_{f}\Omega=\Omega,

(9.8)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\kappa^{-1}_{a}}(\Phi)\Omega=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{((T_{1}a)_{0}^{+})}\Omega

\displaystyle\qquad\text{whenever}\quad A_{v}\Omega=\Omega,

(9.9)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\lambda^{-1}_{a}}(\Phi)\Omega=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{((T_{1}a)_{2}^{-})}\Omega

\displaystyle\qquad\text{whenever}\quad B_{f}\Omega=\Omega,

(9.10)

where
\kappa_{a}
,
\lambda_{a}
are the left ribbons defined in (
8.1
) and (
8.2
).

Proof.

Formulas (
9.5
) and (
9.6
) can be proven as in (
9.3
) and (
9.4
).
The proof of (
9.7
) goes as follows.

\kappa_{a}
contains
\alpha_{a}^{-1}
except one arrow,
\bigl(T_{0}^{m}a\bigr)_{0}^{-}=a_{0}^{-}
. So we write its opholonomy as

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\kappa_{a}}(\Phi)=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{((T_{1}a)_{2}^{+},a_{0}^{+})}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\alpha_{a}^{-1}}\bigl(\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{2}^{+})}\bigl(\Phi_{(3)}\bigr).

We leave the first term for a while and concentrate on the action of the rest on the vacuum:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\alpha_{a}^{-1}}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{2}^{+})}\bigl(\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)\Omega

\displaystyle{}=G_{a}(S\bigl(h_{(1)}\bigr))\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\xi_{i}\varphi_{(1)}\xi_{j},1\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}\varepsilon\bigl(S(x_{j})h_{(2)}x_{i}\bigr)Q_{a}\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)\Omega

\displaystyle{}=G_{a}(S(h))Q_{a}(\varphi)\Omega\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{Q G})}}{{=}}Q_{a}\bigl(\varphi\leftharpoonup h_{(1)}\bigr)G_{a}\bigl(S\bigl(h_{(2)}\bigr)\bigr)\Omega

\displaystyle{}=Q_{a}(\varphi\leftharpoonup h)\Omega,

where, as usual,
\Phi=h\otimes\varphi
stands for an arbitrary element of
\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
.
In order to express this result in terms of
\Phi
, we remark that

u=\xi_{i}\otimes S(x_{i})\qquad\text{and}\qquad u^{-1}=\xi_{i}\otimes x_{i}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle u^{-1}\rightharpoonup\Phi

\displaystyle=\bigl(h_{(1)}\otimes\xi_{j}\varphi_{(1)}\xi_{k}\bigr)\cdot\bigl\bm{}{\langle}S(x_{k})h_{(2)}x_{j}\otimes\varphi_{(2)},\xi_{i}\otimes x_{i}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}

\displaystyle=\bigl(h_{(1)}\otimes\xi_{j}\varphi_{(1)}\xi_{k}\bigr)\cdot\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\varphi_{(2)},S(x_{k})h_{(2)}x_{j}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}

\displaystyle=h_{(1)}\otimes\varphi_{(4)}\varphi_{(1)}S\bigl(\varphi_{(2)}\bigr)\cdot\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\varphi_{(3)},h_{(2)}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}

\displaystyle=h_{(1)}\otimes\varphi\leftharpoonup h_{(2)}

and
\varphi\leftharpoonup h=(\varepsilon\otimes\operatorname{id})\bigl(u^{-1}\rightharpoonup\Phi\bigr)
, so we have

Q_{a}(\varphi\leftharpoonup h)=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{2}^{+})}\bigl(u^{-1}\rightharpoonup\Phi\bigr).

In this way, we have proven that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\kappa_{a}}(\Phi)\Omega

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{((T_{1}a)_{2}^{+},a_{0}^{+})}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{2}^{+})}\bigl(u^{-1}\rightharpoonup\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)\Omega=

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{((T_{1}a)_{2}^{+},a_{0}^{+},a_{2}^{+},(T_{1}a)_{0}^{+})}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{((T_{1}a)_{0}^{-})}\bigl(u^{-1}\rightharpoonup\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)\Omega.

Now the opcurve in the first term is a conjugate of the central curve
\beta_{a}
which, by (
6.10
) and Lemma
6.7
,
we know to have opholonomy equal to
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{M}}\cdot\bm{\langle}\mbox{\,\rm\_}\,,S_{\mathcal{D}}(u)\bm{\rangle}
. Thus,

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\kappa_{a}}(\Phi)\Omega=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{((T_{1}a)_{0}^{-})}\bigl(S_{\mathcal{D}}(u)u^{-1}\rightharpoonup\Phi\bigr)\Omega.

For C
∗
-Hopf algebras,
S_{\mathcal{D}}(u)=u
, therefore (
9.7
) is proven.

The proof of (
9.8
) goes as follows.
We insert
\bigl(a_{2}^{-},a_{2}^{+}\bigr)
into
\lambda_{a}
in order to complete the incomplete face loop,

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\lambda_{a}}(\Phi)=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{((T_{1}a)_{0}^{-},a_{2}^{-})}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma_{a}}\bigl(\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{0}^{-})}\bigl(\Phi_{(3)}\bigr).

Next we compute the action of the last two terms on the vacuum by using the (inverse of the) exchange relation (
3.29
),

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma_{a}}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{0}^{-})}\bigl(\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)\Omega

\displaystyle{}=\varepsilon\bigl(h_{(1)}\bigr)F_{a}\bigl(\xi_{i}\varphi_{(1)}\xi_{j}\bigr)P_{a}\bigl(S\bigl(S(x_{j})h_{(2)}x_{i}\bigr)\bigr)\varphi_{(2)}(1)

\displaystyle{}=P_{a}\bigl((\xi_{i}\varphi\xi_{j})_{(1)}\rightharpoonup S(x_{i})S(h)x_{j}\bigr)F_{a}\bigl((\xi_{i}\varphi\xi_{j})_{(2)}\bigr)\Omega

\displaystyle{}=P_{a}(\xi_{i}\varphi\xi_{j}\rightharpoonup S(x_{i})S(h)x_{j})\Omega.

Next we simplify the last expression by proving the identity

\displaystyle\xi_{i}\varphi\xi_{j}\rightharpoonup S(x_{i})S(h)x_{j}

\displaystyle{}=\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\Phi_{(1)},\varepsilon\otimes x_{i}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\Phi_{(2)},\xi_{j}\otimes 1\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}\cdot(\xi_{i}\rightharpoonup S(x_{j}))

\displaystyle{}=\bm{\langle}\Phi,(\varepsilon\otimes x_{i})(\xi_{j}\xi_{k}\otimes 1)\bm{\rangle}S(x_{k})\bm{\langle}\xi_{i},S(x_{j})\bm{\rangle}

\displaystyle{}=\bm{\langle}\Phi,\underset{S_{\mathcal{D}}(R_{1})R_{2}=S_{\mathcal{D}}(u)}{\underbrace{(\varepsilon\otimes S(x_{j})(\xi_{j}\otimes 1)}}(\xi_{k}\otimes 1)\bm{\rangle}S(x_{k})

\displaystyle{}=S((\operatorname{id}\otimes\varepsilon)(\Phi\leftharpoonup S_{\mathcal{D}}(u))),

which implies

P_{a}(\xi_{i}\varphi\xi_{j}\rightharpoonup S(x_{i})S(h)x_{j})=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{0}^{-})}(\Phi\leftharpoonup S_{\mathcal{D}}(u)).

Putting together and using that
S_{\mathcal{D}}(u)
is central, we obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\lambda_{a}}(\Phi)\Omega

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(T_{1}a)_{0}^{-},a_{2}^{-})}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(a_{0}^{-})}\bigl(\Phi_{(2)}\leftharpoonup S_{\mathcal{D}}(u)\bigr)\Omega

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(T_{1}a)_{0}^{-},a_{2}^{-},a_{0}^{-})}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\Phi_{(2)},S_{\mathcal{D}}(u)\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{(T_{1}a)_{0}^{-},a_{2}^{-},a_{0}^{-},(T_{1}a)_{2}^{-})}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{((T_{1}a)_{2}^{+})}\bigl(S_{\mathcal{D}}(u)\rightharpoonup\Phi_{(2)}\bigr).

Now the opcurve in the first term is a conjugate of
\beta_{a}^{-1}
therefore it is central and its opholonomy is

\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{M}}\cdot\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\mbox{\,\rm\_}\,,S_{\mathcal{D}}(u)^{-1}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}
by Lemma
6.7
. This immediately implies (
9.8
).

Since
\kappa_{a}
,
\lambda_{a}
are proper ribbons, (
9.9
) and (
9.10
) for all
\Phi
are equivalent to (
9.7
)
and (
9.8
), respectively, by (
7.5
).
∎

As a generalization of the protected space, let us introduce the spaces
\mathscr{P}(F)\subseteq\mathscr{H}
of partial vacua as follows. Consider
F\subseteq\Sigma
as a
set of faces of
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
and let
F^{d}:=F\cap\Sigma^{d}
. Then define

\mathscr{P}(F):=\bigl\{\Omega\in\mathscr{H}\mid A_{v}\Omega=\Omega,B_{f}\Omega=\Omega,\ \text{for}\ v\in F^{0},\,f\in F^{2}\bigr\}.

So in particular
\mathscr{P}(\Sigma)=\mathscr{P}
and
\mathscr{P}(\varnothing)=\mathscr{H}
.

Theorem 9.2

(topological invariance)
.

For a connected OCPM
\Sigma
, let
\rho_{1}
,
\rho_{2}
be proper ribbons on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
of the same left/right type and let

F\subset\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*2}
. Then

(i)

if there is a proper ribbon-homotopy
\rho_{1}\to\rho_{2}
of support
F
, then

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho_{1}}(\Phi)\Omega=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho_{2}}(\Phi)\Omega\qquad\text{for all}\quad\Phi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*},\quad\Omega\in\mathscr{P}(F);

(ii)

if
\rho_{1}
,
\rho_{2}
are closed and there is a proper free ribbon-homotopy
\rho_{1}\to\rho_{2}
of support
F
then

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho_{1}}(\Psi)\Omega=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho_{2}}(\Psi)\Omega\qquad\text{for all}\quad\Psi\in\operatorname{Cocom}\mathcal{D}(H)^{*},\quad\Omega\in\mathscr{P}(F).

Proof.

(i) It suffices to consider elementary ribbon-homotopies
(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
in which a subribbon
\sigma_{1}\subset\gamma\sigma_{1}\delta=\rho_{1}
is replaced
by the subribbon
\sigma_{2}\subset\gamma\sigma_{2}\delta=\rho_{2}
. (So
\sigma_{1}\mapsto\sigma_{2}
is either a vertex or face or
\kappa
- or

\lambda
-contraction/relaxation.) Then the argument is this

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho_{1}}(\Phi)\Omega

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\sigma_{1}}\bigl(\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\delta}\bigl(\Phi_{(3)}\bigr)\Omega=[\rho_{1}\text{ is proper}]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\delta}\bigl(\Phi_{(3)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\sigma_{1}}\bigl(\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)\Omega=[\text{Proposition~\ref{pro: contract on vac}}]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\delta}\bigl(\Phi_{(3)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\sigma_{2}}\bigl(\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)\Omega=[\rho_{2}\ \text{is proper}]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\sigma_{2}}\bigl(\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\delta}\bigl(\Phi_{(3)}\bigr)\Omega=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho_{2}}(\Phi)\Omega.

(ii) follows from (i) if we add the observation that for cocommutative
\Psi
we can cyclically permute proper ribbons already at the level of operators:

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho_{1}}(\Psi)=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho_{2}}(\Psi)
whenever
(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
is a circular move, see (
7.6
).
∎

Corollary 9.3
.

Let
\gamma
be a closed proper ribbon,
\rho\colon\bm{\langle}v_{0},f_{0}\bm{\rangle}\to\bm{\langle}v_{1},f_{1}\bm{\rangle}
any proper ribbon and let
\Psi\in\operatorname{Cocom}\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
and
\Phi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
.

(i)

If
\Omega\in\mathscr{P}
,then
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma}(\Psi)\Omega
also belongs to
\mathscr{P}
and depends only on the proper free ribbon-homotopy class of
\gamma
.

(ii)

If
\gamma
is properly contractible
(
Theorem
\ref{thm: proper contract})
and
\Omega\in\mathscr{P}
, then

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma}(\Psi)\Omega=\Omega\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}(\Psi)
.

(iii)

If
\Omega\in\mathscr{P}
, then
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)\Omega
belongs to
\mathscr{P}(\Sigma\setminus\{v_{0},v_{1},f_{0},f_{1}\})
and depends only on the proper ribbon-homotopy
class of
\rho
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Corollary
7.5
and Theorem
9.2
with the additional remark that
in (iii) the support of any proper ribbon-homotopy
\rho\to\rho^{\prime}
is automatically disjoint from
\{v_{0},v_{1},f_{0},f_{1}\}
.
∎

The natural question that emerges is whether the proper ribbon-homotopy classes are the same as the ribbon-homotopy classes or the homotopy classes.
If
\Sigma
is finite and has genus
\mathbf{g}>0
, then the answer is no. While the latter two are infinite groupoids there are only finitely many proper ribbons on a finite
\Sigma
. In addition, proper ribbons cannot be composed so the proper ribbon-homotopy classes need not even form a groupoid.
On the infinite plane, however, one expects that every ribbon-homotopy class contains a proper ribbon so all the three should reduce to the pair groupoid
on the set of sites.

10
Excited states

As explained in
[
12
]
, the holonomy of an open ribbon
\rho\colon s_{0}\to s_{1}
can be thought to create some charge at
s_{1}
and the anticharge
at
s_{0}
. These charges can then be measured by contractible closed ribbon operators winding around
s_{i}
. We can now make this precise in the Hopf
algebraic Kitaev model.

Theorem 10.1
.

Let
\Sigma
be a connected OCPM. On
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*}
let
\rho\colon s_{0}\to s_{1}
be a proper left ribbon and
\gamma\colon s\to s
a closed proper left ribbon.

(i)

We assume that
\gamma
is properly contractible in the sense of Theorem
8.10
so divides
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma)^{*2}
into two
connected components
L
and
R
among which now
L
is the disk.

(ii)

L
contains both faces of the site
s_{1}=\bm{\langle}v_{1},f_{1}\bm{\rangle}
.

(iii)

R
contains both faces of the site
s_{0}=\bm{\langle}v_{0},f_{0}\bm{\rangle}
.

Then for all partial vacuum vectors
\Omega\in\mathscr{P}(L)

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma}(\Psi)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)\Omega=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\leftrightarrow$}}{\Psi}\rightharpoonup\Phi)\Omega

(10.1)

for all
\Phi\in\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
and
\Psi\in\operatorname{Cocom}\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
where the linear isomorphism
\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}\to\mathcal{D}(H)
,

\Psi=k\otimes\psi\mapsto\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\leftrightarrow$}}{\Psi}:=\psi\otimes k
maps
\operatorname{Cocom}\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
onto
\operatorname{Center}\mathcal{D}(H)
.

Proof.

L
is upper semiclosed and contains
v_{1}
and
f_{1}
. The smallest upper semiclosed subcomplex of
\Sigma
which contains
v_{1}
and
f_{1}
is nothing but
\uparrow\!v_{1}
consisting
4
4

4

An example of a
\uparrow\!v
is the hole in the annulus of Figure
9
.

of
v_{1}
and of its vertex neighbourhood
\operatorname{Nb}(v_{1})
(Definition
2.3
). So
\uparrow\!v_{1}\,\subseteq L
and its boundary left
ribbon
\delta
is proper. The set
A
of cells between
\delta
and
\gamma
is an annulus as in Theorem
8.14
so there is a proper
free ribbon-homotopy
\gamma\to\delta
of support
A
. Since
L\supseteq A
,
\Omega\in\mathscr{P}(L)\subseteq\mathscr{P}(A)
and by Corollary

7.5
also
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)\Omega\in\mathscr{P}(A)
. Using topological invariance in the sense of Theorem
9.2
(ii), we have

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma}(\Psi)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)\Omega=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\delta}(\Psi)\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)\Omega.

Now using topological invariance, this time in the sense of Theorem
9.2
(i), of
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)\Omega
, we can choose

\rho
to be as simple inside
\delta
as possible. By proper ribbon-homotopy supported in
L
, we can deform
\rho
in such a way that only the last arrow of the new

\rho
lies

inside
\delta
and the last two arrows of this
\rho
form a straight curve intersecting
\delta
only at a single site. So we can assume
that
\rho
and
\delta
have the following form:

\rho=\rho^{\prime}\bigl((T_{0}a)_{0}^{-},a_{2}^{+}\bigr),\qquad\delta=\bigl(a_{0}^{-}\bigr)\delta^{\prime}\bigl(\bigl(T_{2}^{-1}a\bigr)_{2}^{+}\bigr),

where
a
is chosen such that
s(a)
is the intersection point of
\rho
and
\delta
, hence
s_{1}=s(T_{2}a)
. Notice that we have chosen the base point
of
\delta
to be the intersection point. The
\rho^{\prime}
and
\delta^{\prime}
are proper ribbons with holonomies that commute with that of all other pieces
of opcurves. Now we can compute the exchange relation of
H_{\delta}(\Psi)=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\delta}(\Psi)
and
H_{\rho}(\Phi)=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}(\Phi)
as follows:

\displaystyle H_{\delta}(\Psi)H_{\rho}(\Phi)={}

\displaystyle H_{(a_{0}^{-})}\bigl(\Psi_{(1)}\bigr)H_{\delta^{\prime}}\bigl(\Psi_{(2)}\bigr)H_{((T_{2}^{-1}a)_{2}^{+})}\bigl(\Psi_{(3)}\bigr)

\displaystyle{}\times H_{\rho^{\prime}}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)H_{((T_{0}a)_{0}^{-})}\bigl(\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)H_{(a_{2}^{+})}\bigl(\Phi_{(3)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{ophol exch rel2})}}{{=}}{}

\displaystyle H_{\rho^{\prime}}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)H_{(a_{0}^{-})}\bigl(\Psi_{(1)}\bigr)H_{\delta^{\prime}}\bigl(\Psi_{(2)}\bigr)

\displaystyle{}\times H_{((T_{0}a)_{0}^{-})}\bigl(R_{2}\rightharpoonup\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)H_{((T_{2}^{-1}a)_{2}^{+})}\bigl(R_{1}\rightharpoonup\Psi_{(3)}\bigr)H_{(a_{2}^{+})}\bigl(\Phi_{(3)}\bigr)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle H_{\rho^{\prime}}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)H_{((T_{0}a)_{0}^{-})}\bigl(R_{2}\rightharpoonup\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)H_{(a_{0}^{-})}\bigl(\Psi_{(1)}\bigr)H_{(a_{2}^{+})}\bigl(\Phi_{(3)}\bigr)

\displaystyle{}\times H_{\delta^{\prime}}\bigl(\Psi_{(2)}\bigr)H_{((T_{2}^{-1}a)_{2}^{+})}\bigl(R_{1}\rightharpoonup\Psi_{(3)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{ophol exch rel1})}}{{=}}{}

\displaystyle H_{\rho^{\prime}}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)H_{((T_{0}a)_{0}^{-})}\bigl(R_{2}\rightharpoonup\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)H_{(a_{2}^{+})}\bigl(\Phi_{(3)}\leftharpoonup R_{1^{\prime}}\bigr)H_{(a_{0}^{-})}\bigl(\Psi_{(1)}\leftharpoonup R_{2^{\prime}}\bigr)

\displaystyle{}\times H_{\delta^{\prime}}\bigl(\Psi_{(2)}\bigr)H_{((T_{2}^{-1}a)_{2}^{+})}\bigl(R_{1}\rightharpoonup\Psi_{(3)}\bigr)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle H_{\rho^{\prime}}\bigl(\Phi_{(1)}\bigr)H_{((T_{0}a)_{0}^{-})}\bigl(\Phi_{(2)}\bigr)\!\cdot\!\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\Phi_{(3)},R_{2}R_{1^{\prime}}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}\!\cdot H_{(a_{2}^{+})}\bigl(\Phi_{(4)}\bigr)\!\cdot H_{\delta}(R_{1}\rightharpoonup\Psi\leftharpoonup R_{2^{\prime}}).

The result is reminiscent of (
7.22
) but now, since the endpoints of
\rho
and
\delta
are different, a term
\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\Phi_{(3)},R_{2}R_{1^{\prime}}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}

is wedged into the holonomy of
\rho
which forbids to write the result as
H_{\rho}
of something times
H_{\delta}
of something. Fortunately, letting this
operator acting on
\Omega
will eliminate the problem. Applying Theorem
9.2
(ii) to the properly contractible
\delta
within the disk
L

and using that
\Omega\in\mathscr{P}(L)
, we get

\displaystyle R_{2}R_{1^{\prime}}\otimes\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\delta}(R_{1}\rightharpoonup\Psi\leftharpoonup R_{2^{\prime}})\Omega

\displaystyle{}=R_{2}R_{1^{\prime}}\otimes\Omega\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathcal{D}^{*}}(R_{1}\rightharpoonup\Psi\leftharpoonup R_{2^{\prime}})

\displaystyle{}=R_{2}R_{1^{\prime}}\otimes\Omega\cdot\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\Psi,R_{2^{\prime}}R_{1}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}.

Substituting the definition of the
R
-matrix, we see that
R_{2}R_{1^{\prime}}\otimes R_{2^{\prime}}R_{1}=(\xi_{i}\otimes x_{j})\otimes(\xi_{j}\otimes x_{i})
, therefore

R_{2}R_{1^{\prime}}\bm{\langle}\Psi,R_{2^{\prime}}R_{1}\bm{\rangle}=\smash{\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\leftrightarrow$}}{\Psi}}.

Thus, the wedge term is simply
\bigl\bm{}{\langle}\Phi_{(3)},\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\leftrightarrow$}}{\Psi}\bigr\bm{}{\rangle}
. If we knew that
\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\leftrightarrow$}}{\Psi}
is a central element
of
\mathcal{D}(H)
, then the wedge term would be shiftable along
\rho
until the endpoint and this would prove (
10.1
).
So it remains to show that
\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\leftrightarrow$}}{\Psi}\in\operatorname{Center}\mathcal{D}(H)
if and only if
\Psi\in\operatorname{Cocom}\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
.

Let us introduce the projections
\mathbf{P}
and its transpose
\mathbf{P}^{\mathsf{T}}
by

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}\colon\ \mathcal{D}(H)\to\mathcal{D}(H),\qquad

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(X):={i_{\mathcal{D}}}_{(1)}XS_{\mathcal{D}}\bigl({i_{\mathcal{D}}}_{(2)}\bigr),

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}^{\mathsf{T}}\colon\ \mathcal{D}(H)^{*}\to\mathcal{D}(H)^{*},\qquad

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}^{\mathsf{T}}(\Phi):={i_{\mathcal{D}}}_{(1)}\rightharpoonup\Phi\leftharpoonup S_{\mathcal{D}}\bigl({i_{\mathcal{D}}}_{(2)}\bigr),

where
i_{\mathcal{D}}:=\iota\otimes i
is the Haar integral of
\mathcal{D}(H)
.
\mathbf{P}
projects onto
\operatorname{Center}(\mathcal{D}(H))
and
\mathbf{P}^{\mathsf{T}}
onto
\operatorname{Cocom}\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
.
Using the identity
R\Delta_{\mathcal{D}}(X)=\Delta^{\mathrm{op}}(X)R
of
R
-matrices, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}(R_{2}R_{1^{\prime}})\otimes R_{2^{\prime}}R_{1}

\displaystyle={i_{\mathcal{D}}}_{(1)}R_{2}R_{1^{\prime}}S_{\mathcal{D}}\bigl({i_{\mathcal{D}}}_{(2)}\bigr)\otimes R_{2^{\prime}}R_{1}

\displaystyle={i_{\mathcal{D}}}_{(1)}R_{2}R_{1^{\prime}}S_{\mathcal{D}}\bigl({i_{\mathcal{D}}}_{(4)}\bigr)\otimes R_{2^{\prime}}S_{\mathcal{D}}\bigl({i_{\mathcal{D}}}_{(3)}\bigr){i_{\mathcal{D}}}_{(2)}R_{1}

\displaystyle=R_{2}{i_{\mathcal{D}}}_{(2)}R_{1^{\prime}}S_{\mathcal{D}}\bigl({i_{\mathcal{D}}}_{(4)}\bigr)\otimes R_{2^{\prime}}S_{\mathcal{D}}\bigl({i_{\mathcal{D}}}_{(3)}\bigr)R_{1}{i_{\mathcal{D}}}_{(1)}

\displaystyle=R_{2}{i_{\mathcal{D}}}_{(2)}S_{\mathcal{D}}\bigl({i_{\mathcal{D}}}_{(3)}\bigr)R_{1^{\prime}}\otimes S_{\mathcal{D}}\bigl({i_{\mathcal{D}}}_{(4)}\bigr)R_{2^{\prime}}R_{1}{i_{\mathcal{D}}}_{(1)}

\displaystyle=R_{2}R_{1^{\prime}}\otimes\mathbf{P}(R_{2^{\prime}}R_{1}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\leftrightarrow$}}{\Psi}\in\operatorname{Center}\mathcal{D}(H)\quad

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathbf{P}(\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\leftrightarrow$}}{\Psi})=\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\leftrightarrow$}}{\Psi}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathbf{P}(R_{2}R_{1^{\prime}})\bm{\langle}\Psi,R_{2^{\prime}}R_{1}\bm{\rangle}=R_{2}R_{1^{\prime}}\bm{\langle}\Psi,R_{2^{\prime}}R_{1}\bm{\rangle}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad R_{2}R_{1^{\prime}}\bm{\langle}\mathbf{P}^{T}(\Psi),R_{2^{\prime}}R_{1}\bm{\rangle}=R_{2}R_{1^{\prime}}\bm{\langle}\Psi,R_{2^{\prime}}R_{1}\bm{\rangle}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\mathbf{P}^{\mathsf{T}}(\Psi)=\Psi.

So
\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\leftrightarrow$}}{\Psi}
is central if and only if
\Psi
is tracial and the theorem is proven.
∎

Let
D_{r}
be fixed irreducible unitary representations of
\mathcal{D}(H)
for each isomorphism class
r
of irreps. Then the set of matrix elements
\bigl\{D_{r}^{i,j}\bigr\}

is a basis of
\mathcal{D}(H)^{*}
. For a proper left ribbon
\rho\colon s(a)\to s(b)
between disjoint sites, the (
7.9
) and (
7.10
) imply that
the vectors

\Lambda_{r}^{i,j}:=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}\bigl(D_{r}^{i,j}\bigr)\Omega\in\mathscr{H}

are irreducible
\mathcal{D}(H)
-multiplets in the sense of the equations

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{c}(X)\Lambda_{r}^{i,k}=\Lambda_{r}^{i,k}\cdot\varepsilon_{\mathcal{D}}(X)\qquad\text{if}\quad c\neq a,b,

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{b}(X)\Lambda_{r}^{i,k}=\Lambda_{r}^{i,j}\cdot D_{r}^{j,k}(X),

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{a}(X)\Lambda_{r}^{i,k}=D_{r}^{i,j}(S_{\mathcal{D}}(X))\cdot\Lambda_{r}^{j,k}

provided
\Omega\in\mathscr{P}
. Notice that
\varepsilon_{\mathcal{D}}=D_{0}
if
r=0
designates the trivial representation and
D^{\mathsf{T}}_{r}\,\circ\,S_{\mathcal{D}}
is, up to unitary
equivalence, some
D_{\bar{r}}
if
r\mapsto\bar{r}
designates charge conjugation.

Let
\gamma
be a closed proper ribbon winding around
s(b)
but not
s(a)

as in Theorem
10.1
. Let
\Psi_{r}
be the unique cocommutative element for which
\overset{\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\scriptsize$\leftrightarrow$}}{\Psi}_{r}

is the minimal central idempotent
e_{r}=e_{r}^{i,i}
, where
\bigl\{e_{r}^{i,j}\bigr\}
is the dual basis of
\bigl\{D_{r}^{i,j}\bigr\}
. Then

\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma}(\Psi_{q})\Lambda_{r}^{i,j}=\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\rho}\bigl(e_{q}\rightharpoonup D_{r}^{i,j}\bigr)\Omega=\Lambda^{i,j}_{q}\cdot\delta_{q,r}.

In this sense, the closed ribbon operators
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma}(\Psi)
are able to detect the charge
r
inside the contraction disk of
\gamma
.
This happens in spite of the fact that for whatever closed
\gamma
for which the ribbon-homotopy class of
\rho
contains a
\rho^{\prime}
disjoint from
\gamma

the
\operatorname{Hol}^{\circ}_{\gamma}(\Psi)

detects only the trivial charge on
\Lambda_{r}^{i,j}
. One says that the charge of
\Lambda_{r}^{i,j}
can be localized in semi-infinite strings but not
in a finite region.
This kind of charges are characteristic of massive gauge theories
[
2
]
. This is the first evidence of that the Kitaev model is like
a gauge theory. The more precise analysis should chart the superselection sectors similarly to the Doplicher–Haag–Roberts theory

[
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
]
but for string localized charges. In the abelian Kitaev model on a square lattice such a program can be carried out
[
4
,
19
]

but for arbitrary f.d. C
∗
-Hopf algebra
H
and for arbitrary infinite (planar) OCPM
\Sigma
it is not yet in sight.
